\(\displaystyle \| \mathcal{C}_t f \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 \lesssim_{\mu} \int_0^t \| C_s U_s f \|_{\mathfrak{h}}^2 \mathrm{d} s, \qquad \| \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f \|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 \lesssim_{\mu} \int_0^t \| C_s \Theta f \|_{\mathfrak{h}}^2 \mathrm{d} s,\)

and furthermore

\(\displaystyle \langle \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_s g, \mathcal{C}_t f \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = - \langle \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f, \mathcal{C}_s g \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = (\mu^2 - \mu^{- 2})^{- 1} \langle \Theta g, G_{s \wedge t} f \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} .\)

We abridge our notation to \(\gamma \equiv \gamma_{\varrho = \mu \mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}}}\) and introduce GBMs as follows.

Definition 3.16. Let \((\mathcal{M}^{(\mu)}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t^{(\mu)})_{t \geqslant 0})\) be as above and, let \((G_t)_t\) be a differentiable family of bounded \(\Theta\)-symmetric operators on \(\mathfrak{h}\). Letting \(\mathcal{C}_t\) and \(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t\) be as in Lemma 3.15, we define the process \(X_t : \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{M}_t^{(\mu)}\)

\(\displaystyle X_t (f) := \gamma^{\ast} (\mathcal{C}_t f) - \gamma (\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f) . \) (3.12)

Proposition 3.17. The process \(X_t\) of Definition 3.16 is a GBM valued in \(\mathcal{M}_a^{(\mu)} \cap \mathcal{M}^{(\mu)}_t\), with covariance \(G_t\) and satisfies

\(\displaystyle \| X_{s, t} (f) \|^2 \lesssim_{\mu} \int_s^t (\| C_r U_r f \|_{\mathfrak{h}}^2 + \| C_r \Theta f \|_{\mathfrak{h}}^2) \mathrm{d} r. \) (3.13)

Proof. That \(X\) is adapted is clear by inspection. We check anticommutation relations by (3.2) and by Lemma 3.15

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \{ X_t (f), X_s (g) \} & = & - (\{ \gamma^{\ast} (\mathcal{C}_t f), \gamma (\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_s g) \} + \{ \gamma (\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f), \gamma^{\ast} (\mathcal{C}_s g) \})\\ & = & - (\mu^2 + \mu^{- 2}) (\langle \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_s g, \mathcal{C}_t f \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} + \langle \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f, \mathcal{C}_s g \rangle_{\mathcal{H}}) = 0. \end{array}\)

Furthermore, \(X_t\) are Gaussian random variables because \(\omega\) is quasi-free on \(\gamma\)'s. We compute the covariance with the aid of (3.3) and Lemma 3.15

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega (X_t (f) X_s (g)) & = & - (\omega (\gamma^{\ast} (\mathcal{C}_t f) \gamma (\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_s g) + \omega (\gamma (\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f) \gamma^{\ast} (\mathcal{C}_s g))) \\ & = & - \mu^{- 2} \langle \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_s g, \mathcal{C}_t f \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} - \mu^2 \langle \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f, \mathcal{C}_s g \rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\\ & = & \langle \Theta g, G_{s \wedge t} f \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} \end{array}\)

Finally, (3.13) is obtained by Lemma 3.15 and by recalling that \(\| a (f) \|, \| a^{\ast} (f) \| \leqslant \| f \|_{\mathcal{H}}\), for any \(f \in \mathcal{H}\).\(\Box\)

An important special case of GBM is the Grassmann Brownian motion, corresponding to the choice \(G_t = tC^2 U\) with \(U = \mathbb{1} \oplus - \mathbb{1}\) and some bounded \(C \geqslant 0\). In Section 4, we will only consider this special case, while more general GBMs will be used only in Section 5.1. In the former case, we note the following.

Lemma 3.18. If \(X_t\) is as in Definition 3.16, then \([X_t (f)]_{\tau} = \mu^{- 4 \tau} X_t (f)\). Assume furthermore that \(G_t = tC^2 U\) with \([C, \Theta] = [C, U] = 0\). Then, there exist constants \(c_{r, r'}\) and \(c_{r, r'}'\) depending on \(\mu\) and locally bounded in \(r, r' \in \mathbb{R}\) such that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega ([X_t (f)]_r [X_s (g)]_{r'}) & = & c_{r, r'} (s \wedge t) \langle \Theta f, Gg \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}},\\ \omega ([X_t^{\ast} (f)]_r [X_s (g)]_{r'}) & = & c_{r, r'}' (s \wedge t) \langle f, C^2 g \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} . \end{array} \) (3.14)

Moreover, setting \(X_{s, t} := X_t - X_s\) we have \(\| X_{s, t} (f) \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \lesssim_{\mu, p} | t - s |^{\frac{1}{2}} \| Cf \|_{\mathfrak{h}}\) for any \(p \in [1, \infty]\).

Proof. The action of the automorphism group on \(\beta \equiv \beta_{\varrho = \mu \mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{H}}}\), see (3.4) and (3.8) is simply \(\sigma_t (\beta (f)) = \mu^{- 4 \mathrm{i} t} \beta (f)\), hence its analytical continuation is \([\beta (f)]_{\tau} = \mu^{- 4 \tau} \beta (f)\). We can write \(X_t (f) = \beta (f_t \oplus \tilde{f}_t)\), see Lemma 3.15, with \(f_t := \mu \mathcal{C}_t f\) and \(\tilde{f}_t := \mu^{- 1} \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f\) so that \([X_t (f)]_{\tau} = \mu^{- 4 \tau} X_t (f)\). This fact together with Proposition 3.17 implies the first identity in (3.14). The second identity follows by noting that \(\)\(\mathcal{C}_t f \propto_{\mu} \boldsymbol{1}_{[0, t]} \otimes UCf\) and \(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f \propto_{\mu} \boldsymbol{1}_{[0, t]} \otimes \Theta Cf\), so that

\(\displaystyle \omega (X_t^{\ast} (f) X_s (g)) = \mu^{- 2} \langle \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_s g, \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} + \mu^2 \langle \mathcal{C}_t f, \mathcal{C}_s g \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = \frac{\mu^2 + \mu^{- 2}}{\mu^{- 2} - \mu^2} (s \wedge t) \langle f, C^2 g \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} .\)

Regarding the bound, by Hölder's inequality and by (3.13) we have

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (X_{s, t} (f)) \|_p \leqslant \| [X_{s, t} (f)]_{\tau} \| \lesssim_{\mu, \tau} | t - s |^{\frac{1}{2}} \| Cf \|_{\mathfrak{h}},\)

implying the claim.\(\Box\)

On the other hand, the following identity will be useful in Section 5.

Lemma 3.19. Let \(X_t\) be as in Definition 3.16, then

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \langle T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X_t (f_1) \cdots X_t (f_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}), T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X_s (g_1) \cdots X_s (g_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \rangle_{L^2}\\ \qquad = \mu^{- 2 (1 + 4 \tau) n} \langle (f_{1, t} \oplus \tilde{f}_{1, t}) \wedge \cdots \wedge (f_{n, t} \oplus \tilde{f}_{n, t}), (g_{1, s} \oplus \tilde{g}_{1, s}) \wedge \cdots \wedge (g_{n, s} \oplus \tilde{g}_{n, s}) \rangle_{\Gamma_a (\mathcal{K})}, \end{array} \) (3.15)

where \(f_{j, t} := \mu \mathcal{C}_t f_j\) and \(\tilde{f}_{j, t} := \mu^{- 1} \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t f_j\) and where \(g_{j, s} := \mu \mathcal{C}_s g_j\) and \(\tilde{g}_{j, s} := \mu^{- 1} \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_s g_j\).

Proof. We note that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X_t (f_1) \cdots X_t (f_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) & = & [\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta (f_{1, t} \oplus \tilde{f}_{1, t}) \cdots \beta (f_{n, t} \oplus \tilde{f}_{n, t}) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}]_{\frac{1}{4} + \tau} D^{\frac{1}{2}}\\ & = & \mu^{- (1 + 4 \tau) n} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta (f_{1, t} \oplus \tilde{f}_{1, t}) \cdots \beta (f_{n, t} \oplus \tilde{f}_{n, t}) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} D^{\frac{1}{2}} \end{array}\)

and thus, by the definition of Haagerup's trace and by (3.7) we obtain (3.15).\(\Box\)

3.3Hypercontractivity

Let us now recall the hypercontractivity result in [LR11] for the special case of Araki–Wyss factors. Let \(P_t\) be the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) semigroup on \(\mathcal{A} \equiv \mathcal{A} (\mathcal{H}, \varrho)\), defined by

\(\displaystyle P_t (x) \Omega := \mathrm{e}^{- t \mathcal{N}} (x \Omega), \qquad \forall x \in \mathcal{A} (\mathcal{H}, \varrho),\)

where \(\mathcal{N} = \mathrm{d} \Gamma (\mathbb{1})\) is the number operator on \(\Gamma_a (\mathcal{K})\). By (3.6), we have

\(\displaystyle P_t (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (f_1) \cdots \beta_{\varrho} (f_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) = \mathrm{e}^{- tn} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (f_1) \cdots \beta_{\varrho} (f_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}, \) (3.16)

that is, the Wick's polynomials are eigenvectors of the OU semigroup. Furthermore, note that the modular operator \(\Delta\) commutes with the number operator \(\mathcal{N}\) and thus

\(\displaystyle \sigma_s (P_t (x)) = P_t (\sigma_s (x)), \qquad \forall s, t \in \mathbb{R}, \) (3.17)

so that \(P_t\) maps the subalgebra of analytic elements \(\mathcal{A}_a\) into itself. To extend this map to the \(L^p\) spaces, one introduces the densely-defined operators, for \(1 \leqslant p, q \leqslant \infty\),

\(\displaystyle P_t^{(p, q)} : \mathcal{A}D^{\frac{1}{p}} \rightarrow \mathcal{A}D^{\frac{1}{q}}, \qquad xD^{\frac{1}{p}} \mapsto P_t (x) D^{\frac{1}{q}} . \) (3.18)

These operators are not of the form (2.8), and in fact there is no general extension theorem that holds. Still, for suitable \(p, q\), Lee–Ricard prove the following result [LR11].

Theorem 3.20. (Lee–Ricard, 2011). Let \(1 < p < 2\). Then \(P_t^{(p, 2)}\) extends to a contraction from \(L^p (\mathcal{A} (\mathcal{H}, \varrho))\) to \(L^2 (\mathcal{A} (\mathcal{H}, \varrho))\), that is, \(\| P_t^{(p, 2)} \|_{L^p \rightarrow L^2} \leqslant 1\) provided that

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{e}^{- 2 t} \lesssim \| \varrho \|_{\mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}}^{\frac{8}{p} - 4} (p - 1) .\)

Remark 3.21. Note that the map \(P_t^{(p, q)}\) can be more generally defined on \(D^{\frac{1}{2 p} + \tau} \mathcal{A}D^{\frac{1}{2 p} - \tau}\) by

\(\displaystyle P_t^{(p, q)} (T^{(p)}_{\tau} (x)) := T^{(q)}_{\tau} (P_t (x)), \) (3.19)

compare with (2.9), and would not depend on \(\tau\) because of Lemma 2.12 and the state preserving property (2.7). The statement of the theorem holds for any such map as well since in fact \(T^{(p)}_t (x) = [x]_{\frac{1}{2 p} + t} D^{\frac{1}{p}}\) and we by (3.17) we have \([P_t (x)]_s = P_t ([x]_s)\) for \(x \in \mathcal{A}_a\), \(P_t\) mapping \(\mathcal{A}_a\) into itself.

Corollary 3.22. Let \(2 < p < \infty\). Then \(P_t^{(2, p)}\) extends to a contraction from \(L^2\) to \(L^p\) provided that \(\mathrm{e}^{- 2 t} \lesssim \| \varrho \|_{\mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}}^{\frac{8}{p'} - 4} (p' - 1)\), with \(1 / p' + 1 / p = 1\).

Proof. By duality and by the density of \(\mathcal{M}_a D^{\frac{1}{p}}\), we can write, for \(x \in \mathcal{M}_a\) and \(t\) as in the claim

\(\displaystyle \left\| P_t^{(2, p)} \left( xD^{\frac{1}{2}} \right) \right\|_p = \sup_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} y \in \mathcal{M}_a D^{1 / p'},\\ \| y \|_{p'} = 1 \end{array}$}}} \left| \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} \left( y^{\ast} P_t (x) D^{\frac{1}{p}} \right) \right| = \sup_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} y \in \mathcal{M}_a D^{1 / p'},\\ \| y \|_{p'} = 1 \end{array}$}}} \left| \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} \left( P_t^{(p', 2)} (y)^{\ast} xD^{\frac{1}{2}} \right) \right| \leqslant \left\| xD^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_2,\)

where the bound follows by Hölder's inequality and by Theorem 3.20. This implies the claim.\(\Box\)

The following hypercontractive estimates hold true.

Corollary 3.23. There exists a constant \(C_{p, \varrho} \propto \| \varrho \|_{\mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}}^{2 - \frac{4}{p}} (p - 1)^{- \frac{1}{2}}\) such that, for any polynomial \(F \in \Gamma_a (\mathcal{K})\)

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_2 \leqslant C_{p, \varrho}^{\deg (F)} \| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_p \qquad p \in [1, 2), \) (3.20)
\(\displaystyle \| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_p \leqslant C_{\frac{p}{p - 1}, \varrho}^{\deg (F)} \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_2 \qquad p \in (2, \infty] . \) (3.21)

Proof. Take a monomial \(F = f_1 \wedge \cdots \wedge f_n\). Then, by (3.16) and (3.19)

\(\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) = \mathrm{e}^{tn} T^{(2)}_{\tau} (P_t (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}})) = \mathrm{e}^{tn} P_t^{(p, 2)} (T^{(p)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}})) \) (3.22)

and the estimate for \(\| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_2\) follows by Theorem 3.20, by choosing \(\mathrm{e}^t \sim \| \varrho \|_{\mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}}^{2 - \frac{4}{p}} (p - 1)^{- \frac{1}{2}}\). The estimate for \(\| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_p\) follows in the same way. To extend it to general polynomial \(F = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} F_n\) with \(F_n\) monomials, since \(T^{(2)}_{\tau} (x) = [x]_{\frac{1}{4} + \tau} D^{\frac{1}{2}}\) \(\)by (3.7) we have

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_2^2 = \sum_{n, n'} \omega \left( [\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F_{n'}) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}]_{\frac{1}{4} + \tau}^{\ast} [\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}]_{\frac{1}{4} + \tau} \right) = \sum_n \left\| [\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}]_{\frac{1}{4} + \tau} \right\|_2^2, \) (3.23)

which allows one to obtain (3.20) from (3.22). The bound (3.21) follows by writing

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_T (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_p^2 & = & \left\| \sum_n \mathrm{e}^{tn} P^{(2, p)} (T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}})) \right\|^2_p\\ & \leqslant & \left\| \sum_n \mathrm{e}^{tn} T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \right\|^2_2\\ & = & \sum_n \mathrm{e}^{2 tn} \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F_n) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|^2_2\\ & \leqslant & \left( C_{\frac{p}{p - 1}, \varrho}^{\deg (F)} \right)^2 \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \beta_{\varrho} (F) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \|_2^2, \end{array}\)

where in the first and second inequality we used Theorem 3.20 and (3.23).\(\Box\)

4Stochastic calculus

4.1Itô integration

We extend the construction of the Itô–Clifford integral carried out in [BSW82, BSW83b, BSW83c, PX97] to the Grassmann setting. We begin by describing a general theory of integration with respect to \(\mathbb{L}^p\) martingales in Subsections 4.1.1–4.1.2 under some mild assumptions on the martingale and then restrict to the special case of integration in the Grassmann algebra in Subsection 4.1.3. Unlike the case of Clifford random variables, we deal with martingales whose square is not simply a multiple of the identity, property which was crucially used in the former works in order to compute the quadratic variation of the Clifford martingale.

4.1.1Scalar Itô integral

We begin by considering the integration of an \(\mathbb{L}^p\) simple adapted process against an \(\mathbb{L}^q\) martingale.

Definition 4.1. (Itô integral). Let \(q, p \in [1, \infty]\) such that \(1 / p + 1 / q \leqslant 1\). Let \(F_t\) be a simple adapted process in \(\mathbb{L}^p\) as defined in (2.11) and let \(X_t\) be an \(\mathbb{L}^q\) martingale, letting \(X_{s, t} := X_t - X_s\) for \(s \leqslant t\), the Itô integral of \(F\) with respect to \(X\) is the process \(Y = (Y_t)_t\)

\(\displaystyle Y_t = \int_0^t F_s \cdot \mathrm{d} X_s := \sum_{j = 0}^k F_{t_j} \cdot X_{t_j, t_{j + 1} \wedge t}, \) (4.1)

where \(k\) is such that \(t \in [t_k, t_{k + 1})\) and where the product between elements of the twisted spaces was given in Definition 2.17.

Remark 4.2. The Itô integral defines an \(\mathbb{L}^r\) martingale, with \(1 / r = 1 / p + 1 / q\).

In the rest of this section, we will extend the Itô integral to a larger class of adapted process. Note that in Definition 4.1 we could consider the integration of an \(L^p\) simple adapted process against an \(L^q\) martingale. However, one cannot extend the definition to more general \(L^p\) adapted processes unless the martingale is analytic, stressing once more the relevance of the twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces.

As a warm up, we consider \(\mathbb{L}^2\) valued processes and integration against a martingale in \(\mathcal{M}_a\). We introduce the following subspace of \(\mathbb{H}^p ([0, t])\), see Definition 2.34, for \(p \in [2, \infty)\)

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p ([0, t]) := \left\{ F \in \mathbb{H}^p ([0, t]) \, | \, \int_0^t \| F_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}^2 \mathrm{d} s < \infty \right\} . \) (4.2)

That this is indeed a subspace follows from the fact that for any \(F \in \mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\)

\(\displaystyle \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}^p_{\sharp} ([0, t])}^2 = \sup_{| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 p}} \| T_{\tau}^{(p)} (F) \|_{\mathcal{H}^p_{\sharp} ([0, t])}^2 = \sup_{| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 p}} \left\| \int_0^t | T_{\tau}^{(p)} (F_s)^{\#} |^2 \mathrm{d} s \right\|_{\frac{p}{2}} \leqslant \int_0^t \| F_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}^2 \mathrm{d} s,\)

where we used the shorthand \(F^{\#}\) for \(F\) or \(F^{\ast}\) depending on whether \(\sharp = c\) or \(\sharp = r\).

Proposition 4.3. Let \(F\) be an \(\mathbb{L}^2\) simple adapted process, let \(X\) be a \(\mathcal{M}_a\) martingale with independent increments such that, for any \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant t\)

\(\displaystyle \omega_s ([X_{s, t}^{\ast}]_r [X_{s, t}]_{r'}) = (t - s) c'_{r, r'}, \qquad \forall 0 \leqslant s \leqslant t, \qquad \forall r, r' \in \mathbb{R}, \) (4.3)

for some constant \(c'_{r, r'}\). Let \(Y_t\) denote the Itô integral as in Definition 4.1. Then, for any \(| \tau | \leqslant \frac{3}{4}\) there is a constant \(c_{\tau}\) (independent of \(F\)) such that

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_2^2 = c_{\tau} \int_0^t \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (F_r) \|_2^2 \mathrm{d} r. \) (4.4)

In particular, the Itô integral extends to a map from \(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^2 ([0, t])\) to \(C^0 ([0, t], \mathbb{L}^2)\) and

\(\displaystyle \| Y_t \|^2_{\mathbb{L}^2} \lesssim \int_0^t \| F_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s.\)

Remark 4.4. Note that by Lemma 3.18 the GBM introduced in Definition 3.16 satisfies (4.3), having chosen some direction \(f \in \mathfrak{h}\), or simply taking \(\mathfrak{h}\) one dimensional.

Proof. We fix \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\) and the set \((t_j)_{j \geqslant 0} \subset \mathbb{R}_+\), we let \(k\) be such that \(t \in [t_k, t_{k + 1})\) and set \(\tilde{t}_j = t_j \wedge t\) for \(j = 0, \ldots, k\). We also abridge our notation by introducing \(F_j \equiv F_{\tilde{t}_j}\) and \(\delta X_j \equiv X_{\tilde{t}_{j + 1}} - X_{\tilde{t}_j}\). For \(F_j \in \mathcal{M}_a \cap \mathcal{M}_j\), we have by the definition of Haagerup's trace

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_2^2 =\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} (T^{(2)}_{- \tau} (Y_t^{\ast}) T^{(2)}_{\tau} (Y_t)) = \omega (Y_t^{\ast} [Y_t]_s),\)

where we set \(s = \frac{1}{2} + 2 \tau\). Then, by the modular property we can write

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_2^2 = \sum_{j, j'} \omega (\delta X_j^{\ast} F_j^{\ast} [F_{j'} \delta X_{j'}]_s) = \sum_{j, j'} \omega (F_j^{\ast} [F_{j'}]_s [\delta X_{j'}]_s [\delta X_j^{\ast}]_1) .\)

If \(j' > j\), then we have

\(\displaystyle \omega (F_j^{\ast} [F_{j'}]_s [\delta X_{j'}]_s [\delta X_j^{\ast}]_1) = \omega (F_j^{\ast} [F_{j'}]_s \omega_{j'} ([\delta X_{j'}]_s) [\delta X_j^{\ast}]_1) = 0,\)

since the conditional expectation is state preserving, see (2.7), and since \(X\) is an \(\mathcal{M}_a\) martingale. Similarly, is the expectation vanishing if \(j < j'\). Therefore, by assumption (4.3)

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_2^2 = \sum_j \omega (F_j^{\ast} [F_j]_s) \omega_j (\delta X_j^{\ast} [\delta X_j]_s) = c_{0, s}' \sum_j \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (F_j) \|_2^2 \delta t_j, \) (4.5)

so that, by continuity, this holds true for any \(\mathbb{L}^2\) valued simple adapted \(F\), proving the claim (4.4). Since \(c'_{0, s}\) is locally bounded, we can take the sup over \(\tau\) we obtain \(\| Y_t \|^2_{\mathbb{L}^2} \lesssim \int_0^t \| F_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s\) for any \(F \in \mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\). The conclusion follows by the density of \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\) in \(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^2 ([0, t])\), see Lemma 2.36.\(\Box\)

To extend this result to the general case of integration of an \(\mathbb{L}^p\) adapted process against a suitable \(\mathbb{L}^q\) martingale, we shall now resort to martingales inequalities, see Theorem 2.31. The following is the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.5. Let \(p, q, r \in (2, \infty]\) with \(1 / r \geqslant 1 / p + 1 / q\). Let \(X\) be an \(\mathbb{L}^q\) martingale with independent increments and such that

\(\displaystyle \| X_{s, t} \|_{\mathbb{L}^q} \lesssim (t - s)^{\frac{1}{2}}, \qquad \forall 0 \leqslant s \leqslant t .\) (4.6)

Then, the Itô integral extends to a map \(F \mapsto Y_{\cdot} = \int_0^{\cdot} F_s \cdot \mathrm{d} X_s\) from \(\mathbb{H}_{\operatorname{loc}}^p (\mathbb{R}_+)\) to \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{L}^r)\) and

\(\displaystyle \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^r} \lesssim_r \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}^p ([0, t])} . \) (4.7)

Remark 4.6. Note once more that (4.6) is satisfied by the GBM introduced in Definition 3.16, see Lemma 3.18.

Proof. For the sake of brevity, we denote by \(\mathcal{M}_j \equiv \mathcal{M}_{t_j}\) and write \(F_j := F_{t_j}\), \(X_j := X_{t_j}\), \(\delta X_j := X_{j + 1} - X_j\) and \(\delta Y_j = F_{j - 1} \cdot \delta X_{j - 1}\) for \(j \geqslant 1\) and \(\delta Y_0 := 0\). The martingale property follows by direct inspection, since \(F_j\) is adapted and \(\mathcal{E}^{(q)}_{j'} \left( {\delta X_j} \right) = 0\), \(\forall j' \leqslant j\), being \(X_t\) a martingale.

To prove (4.7), we control the norm \(\| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_r\) at fixed \(\tau\) by means of the Burkholder's inequality (2.16). Thus, we shall bound the norms \(\| \cdot \|_{h^r_c}\), \(\| \cdot \|_{h^r_r}\) and \(\| \cdot \|_{h^r_d}\). First of all, we consider \(F_j, X_j \in \mathcal{M}_a \cap \mathcal{M}_j\) and note that

\(\displaystyle | T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2 = D^{\frac{1}{2 r} - \tau} \delta X_j^{\ast} F^{\ast}_j [F_j \delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} D^{\frac{3}{2 r} + 2 \tau} .\)

Thus, by definition of \(\omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)}\) and by the properties of \(\omega_j\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)} (| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2) & = & {D^{\frac{1}{2 r} - \tau}} \omega_j \left( \delta X_j^{\ast} F^{\ast}_j [F_j \delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right) D^{\frac{3}{2 r} + \tau}\\ & = & {D^{\frac{1}{2 r} - \tau}} \omega_j \left( F^{\ast}_j [F_j \delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} [\delta X_j^{\ast}]_1 \right) D^{\frac{3}{2 r} + \tau}\\ & = & | T^{(r)}_{\tau} (F_j) |^2 \omega_j \left( \delta X_j^{\ast} [\delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right), \end{array}\)

where we used that \(F_j, F_j^{\ast} \in \mathcal{M}_j\) and that \(\omega_j \left( \delta X_j^{\ast} [\delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right) \in \mathbb{C}\), \(X\) having independent increments. The latter property also implies

\(\displaystyle \left\| \omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)} (| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta X_j) |^2) \right\|_{\frac{r}{2}} = \left\| D^{\frac{1}{2 r} - \tau} \omega_j \left( \delta X_j^{\ast} [\delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right) D^{\frac{3}{2 r} + \tau} \right\|_{\frac{r}{2}} = \left| \omega_j \left( \delta X_j^{\ast} [\delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right) \right| .\)

Accordingly, for any \(| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 r}\), by Proposition 2.22 and by Hölder's inequality we have, recalling that \(r \leqslant q\)

\(\displaystyle \left| \omega_j \left( \delta X_j^{\ast} [\delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right) \right| \leqslant \| | T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta X_j) | \|_r^2 \leqslant \| \delta X_j \|_{\mathbb{L}^q}^2 .\)

Since by Proposition 2.22 \(\omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)} (| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2) \geqslant 0\), we obtain

\(\displaystyle \omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)} (| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2) \leqslant \sum_{j \geqslant 0} | T^{(r)}_{\tau} (F_j) |^2 \| \delta X_j \|_{\mathbb{L}^q}^2, \) (4.8)

which can be extended by continuity to \(X_j \in \mathbb{L}^q\). All in all, by assumption (4.6) we have

\(\displaystyle \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)} (| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2) \lesssim \sum_{j \geqslant 0} | T^{(r)}_{\tau} (F_j) |^2 \delta t_j = D^{\frac{1}{q}} \left( \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \left| T^{(p)}_{\tau + \frac{1}{2 q}} (F_j ) \right|^2 \delta t_j \right) D^{\frac{1}{q}}\)

We observe that

\(\displaystyle \left( D^{\frac{1}{q}} \left( \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \left| T^{(p)}_{\tau + \frac{1}{2 q}} (F_j ) \right|^2 \delta t_j \right) D^{\frac{1}{q}} \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} = \left| \left( \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \left| T^{(p)}_{\tau + \frac{1}{2 q}} (F_j ) \right|^2 \delta t_j \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} D^{\frac{1}{q}} \right|,\)

and thus, since \(\| | x | \|_r = \| x \|_r\), by Hölder's inequality we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_{h^r_c} & \lesssim & \left\| \left( D^{\frac{1}{q}} \left( \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \left| T^{(p)}_{\tau + \frac{1}{2 q}} (F_j ) \right|^2 \delta t_j \right) D^{\frac{1}{q}} \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_r = \left\| \left| \left( \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \left| T^{(p)}_{\tau + \frac{1}{2 q}} (F_j ) \right|^2 \delta t_j \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} D^{\frac{1}{q}} \right| \right\|_r\\ & \leqslant & \left\| \left( \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \left| T^{(p)}_{\tau + \frac{1}{2 q}} (F_j ) \right|^2 \delta t_j \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_p . \end{array} \) (4.9)

We observe that if \(| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 r}\), clearly \(\left| \tau + \frac{1}{2 q} \right| \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 r} + \frac{1}{2 q} = 1 - \frac{1}{2 p}\), hence

\(\displaystyle \sup_{| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 r}} \| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_{h^r_c} \lesssim \sup_{| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 p}} \left\| \left( \int_0^t | T^{(p)}_{\tau} (F_s ) |^2 \mathrm{d} s \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_p \equiv \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}_c^p ([0, t])},\)

and similarly for the \(\| \cdot \|_{h^r_r}\) norm. We control the diagonal Hardy norm by using Hölder's inequality for the twisted spaces and the monotonicity of \(x \mapsto x^{\frac{1}{r}}\) and \(x \mapsto x^r\) for \(x \in \mathbb{R}_+\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\tau} \left( \sum_j \| \delta Y_j \|^r_r \right)^{\frac{1}{r}} \leqslant \left( \sum_j (\sup_{\tau} \| \delta Y_j \|_r)^r \right)^{\frac{1}{r}} = \left( \sum_j \| \delta Y_j \|_{\mathbb{L}^r}^r \right)^{\frac{1}{r}} \lesssim \left( \sum_j \| F_j \|^r_{\mathbb{L}^p} \delta t_j^{\frac{r}{2}} \right)^{\frac{1}{r}},\)

which can be made as small as desirable for \(r > 2\). Thus, the \(h^p\) norm is dominated by the contribution \(h^p_{\sharp}\) for \(\sharp = c, r\), and the claim follows by the Burkholder's inequality

\(\displaystyle \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^r} \lesssim_p \max_{\sharp = c, r} \sup_{| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 r}} \| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_{h^r_{\sharp}} \lesssim \max_{\sharp = c, r} \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}_{\sharp}^p ([0, t])} = \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}_{\operatorname{loc}}^p ([0, t])} . \)

By Lemma 2.36, \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{H}^p\) hence (4.9) extends by continuity to any \(F \in \mathbb{H}^p ([0, t])\).\(\Box\)

Remark 4.7. It is possible to modify the hypotheses of Theorem 4.5, in such a way to consider processes \(X\) satisfying inequality (4.6) without having independent increments. Indeed, if we consider a martingale \(X\) such that \(\| X_{s, t} \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} \lesssim (t - s)^{\frac{1}{2}}\) and \(F \in \tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{\operatorname{loc}}\), for some \(p \in (2, \infty]\), see (4.2) then \(Y_t = \int_0^t F_s \cdot \mathrm{d} X_s\) is well-defined and, for every \(r \in (2, p]\), \(\| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^r} \lesssim \| F \|_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p ([0, t])}\). Under the described hypotheses, for any simple process \(F \in \tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{\operatorname{loc}}\), we get

\(\displaystyle \left\| \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)} (| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^r} \lesssim \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \| F_j \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \delta t_j,\)

where we used the fact that \(\left\| \omega_j \left( \delta X_j^{\ast} [\delta X_j]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right) \right\|_{L^{\infty}} \leqslant \| \delta X_j \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^2 \lesssim \delta t_j\).

4.1.2Multidimensional Itô integral

We shall now consider martingales and adapted processes indexed in a Hilbert space \(\mathfrak{h}\) with conjugation \(\Theta\). We begin with the following definition.

Definition 4.8. Let \(p \in [1, + \infty]\). We say that \(F\) is a simple adapted process in \(\mathbb{L}^p\) indexed in the Hilbert space \(\mathfrak{h}\) if \(F : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathbb{L}^p\) such that for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), the map \(v \mapsto F_t (v)\), \(v \in \mathfrak{h}\), is linear, and for any \(v \in \mathfrak{h}\), the process \(t \mapsto F_t (v)\) is simple and adapted. Furthermore, we say that \(F \) is an \(\mathbb{L}^p\) finite-dimensional simple adapted process, if furthermore there exists a set \(\{ v_1, \ldots, v_k \} \subset \mathfrak{h}\) such that \(F_t (v) = 0\) for any \(v \in \operatorname{span} \{ v_1, \ldots, v_k \}^{\bot}\).

For finite-dimensional simple adapted processes the multidimensional Itô integral is the following generalisation of Definition 4.1.

Definition 4.9. Let \(q, p \in [1, \infty]\) such that \(1 / p + 1 / q \leqslant 1\). Let \(F\) be an \(\mathbb{L}^p\) finite-dimensional simple adapted process and \(X_t\) an \(\mathbb{L}^q\) martingale, both indexed in the Hilbert space \(\mathfrak{h}\). The multidimensional Itô integral of \(F\) with respect to \(X\) is the process \(Y = (Y_t)_t\)

\(\displaystyle Y_t = \int_0^t \langle F_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle := \sum_{\alpha} \int_0^t F_s (v_{\alpha}) \cdot \mathrm{d} X_s (v_{\alpha}),\)

where \(\{ v_{\alpha} \}_{\alpha \in \mathbb{N}}\) is any orthonormal real basis with respect the conjugation \(\Theta\) and where the r.h.s. is the scalar Itô integral of Definition 4.1.

Remark 4.10. Note that \(Y\) does not depend on the choice of the orthonormal real basis \(\{ v_{\alpha} \}_{a \in \mathbb{N}}\).

Notation 4.11. Let \(A : \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathfrak{h}\) be a bounded linear operator and \(F : \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathbb{L}^p\), \(H : \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathbb{L}^q\), for some \(q, p \geqslant 2\), be some linear (or anti-linear) maps. We set

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Tr}_A (F \otimes H) := \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \langle Av_{\alpha}, v_{\beta} \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} F (v_{\alpha}) \cdot H (v_{\beta})\)

where \(\{ v_{\alpha} \}_{\alpha \in \mathbb{N}}\) is some orthonormal real basis of \(\mathfrak{h}\) with respect to the conjugation \(\Theta\), whenever the series is absolutely convergent in \(\mathbb{L}^r\), \(1 / r = 1 / p + 1 / q\), in which case it does not depend on the choice of the real orthonormal basis \(\{ v_{\alpha} \}\).

Remark 4.12. When \(A \geqslant 0\) commutes with \(\Theta\), there is \(\tilde{A}\) such that \(\tilde{A}^2 = A\) and for any orthonormal (real) basis \(\{ v_{\alpha} \}\) of h we have \(\langle \tilde{A} v_{\alpha}, v_{\beta} \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} = \langle \tilde{A} v_{\beta}, v_{\alpha} \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} \in \mathbb{R}\) and as a series of positive operators

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Tr}_A (F^{\ast} \otimes F) = \sum_{\alpha} | F (\tilde{A} (v_{\alpha})) |^2 . \) (4.10)

For this reason it is appealing to introduce the notation

\(\displaystyle | F |_A^2 := \operatorname{Tr}_A (F^{\ast} \otimes F) .\)

As long as \(F\) is finite-dimensional, the Itô integral can be extended as was done in the previous section to processes such that \(F (v_{\alpha}) \in \mathbb{H}^p ([0, t])\) for \(\alpha\) in a finite set. Otherwise, we introduce a finer topology in the following way. We let \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}} (\mathfrak{h})\) denote the linear space of finite-dimensional simple adapted processes indexed in the Hilbert space \(\mathfrak{h}\) and taking value in \(\mathcal{M}\)_a. On \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}} (\mathfrak{h})\) we introduce the norms, compare with (2.17)

\(\displaystyle \| F \|_{\mathcal{H}^p_c ([0, t]) ; A} := \left\| \left( \int_0^t | F_s |^2_A \mathrm{d} s \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_p, \qquad \| F \|_{\mathcal{H}^p_r ([0, t]) ; A} := \left\| \left( \int_0^t | F_s^{\ast} |^2_A \mathrm{d} s \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_p . \) (4.11)

We then introduce the following extension of the twisted Hardy spaces of Definition 2.34.

Definition 4.13. Let \(\mathfrak{h}\) be a separable Hilbert space with conjugation \(\Theta\). Let \(A \geqslant 0\) be a bounded linear operator on \(\mathfrak{h}\) commuting with \(\Theta\). For \(\sharp = c, r\) let \(\mathbb{H}^p_{A, \sharp} ([0, t])\) be the completion of \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}} (\mathfrak{h})\) with respect to the twisted norms

\(\displaystyle \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}_{\sharp, A}^p ([0, t])} := \sup_{| \tau | \leqslant 1 - \frac{1}{2 p}} \| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (F) \|_{\mathcal{H}^p_{\sharp} ([0, t]) ; A} .\)

For \(p \in [2, \infty)\) set

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{H}^p_A ([0, t]) := \mathbb{H}_{A, c}^p ([0, t]) \cap \mathbb{H}_{A ; r}^p ([0, t]), \qquad \| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{H}^p_A ([0, t])} := \max_{\sharp = c, r} \| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{H}_{A, \sharp}^p ([0, t])},\)

and let \(\mathbb{H}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+)\) denote the space of processes on \(\mathbb{R}_+\) whose restriction to \([0, t]\) is in \(\mathbb{H}^p ([0, t])\).

Remark 4.14. It is important to note that \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\) is dense also in \(\mathbb{H}^p_A\) and this is a simple generalisation of Corollary 2.36. In fact, it suffices to observe that (2.19), which was used in the proof of the Stein's inequality, holds for \(| \cdot |_A\) as well: by (4.10), we have

\(\displaystyle \left| \sum_j r_j F_j \right|_A^2 = \sum_{\alpha} \left| \sum_j r_j F_j (\tilde{A} v_{\alpha}) \right|^2 \leqslant \sum_{\alpha} \sum_j r_j | F_j (\tilde{A} v_{\alpha}) |^2 = \sum_j r_j | F_j |^2_A .\)

We also introduce the following subspace of \(\mathbb{H}_A^2 ([0, t])\), compare with (4.2)

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\mathbb{H}}_A^2 ([0, t]) := \left\{ F \in \mathbb{H}_A^2 ([0, t]) | \int_0^t \| | F_s |_A \|_{\mathbb{L}^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s < \infty \right\} .\)

We consider integration of an \(\mathbb{L}^p\) adapted process for \(p \geqslant 2\) against a \(\mathcal{M}_a\) martingale. In comparison with Proposition 4.3, note that we consider more general adapted processes, as was done in Theorem 4.5, albeit we still have some strong assumption on the martingale.

Theorem 4.15. Let \(\mathfrak{h}\) be a separable Hilbert space with conjugation \(\Theta\). Let \(X\) be an \(\mathcal{M}_a\) martingale indexed in \(\mathfrak{h}\) such that

\(\displaystyle \omega ([X_{s, t}^{\ast} (f)]_r [X_{s, t} (g)]_{r'}) = (t - s) c'_{r, r'} \langle f, Ag \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}, \qquad \forall 0 \leqslant s \leqslant t, \quad \forall f, g \in \mathfrak{h}, \quad \forall r, r' \in \mathbb{R}, \) (4.12)

for some constant \(c'_{r, r'}\) and some \(A \geqslant 0\) commuting with \(\Theta\). Then, the Itô integral extends to a map \(F \mapsto Y_{\cdot} = \int_0^{\cdot} \langle F_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle\) from \(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}_A^2 ([0, t])\) to \(C^0 ([0, t], \mathbb{L}^2)\) and

\(\displaystyle \| Y_t \|^2_{\mathbb{L}^2} \lesssim \int_0^t \| | F_s |_A \|_{\mathbb{L}^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s.\)

Assume furthermore that \(\| [X_{s, t} (f)]_r \| \lesssim_{r, f} | t - s |^{1 / 2}\). Then, the Itô integral extends to a map \(F \mapsto Y_{\cdot} = \int_0^{\cdot} \langle F_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle\) from \(\mathbb{H}_{A ; \operatorname{loc}}^p (\mathbb{R}_+)\) to \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{L}^p)\) for \(p \in (2, \infty]\) and

\(\displaystyle \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \lesssim_p \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}^p_A ([0, t])} .\)

Proof. In the case of \(p = 2\) the proof of Proposition 4.3 carries over and one obtains

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_2^2 & = & \sum_j \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \omega (F_j^{\ast} (v_{\alpha}) [F_j (v_{\beta})]_s) \omega_j (\delta X_j^{\ast} (v_{\alpha}) [\delta X_j (v_{\beta})]_s)\\ & = & c_{0, s}' \sum_j \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} (T^{(2)}_{\tau} (F_j (v_{\alpha}))^{\ast} T^{(2)}_{\tau} (F_j (v_{\beta}))) \delta t_j \langle v_{\alpha}, Av_{\beta} \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}\\ & = & c_{0, s}' \sum_j \| | T^{(2)}_{\tau} (F_j) |_A^2 \| \delta t_j, \end{array}\)

in place of (4.5), hence the claim. The case \(p \in (2, \infty]\) is an adaptation of the proof of Theorem 4.5. Following the said proof, we obtain the identity

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega_j^{\left( \frac{p}{2} \right)} (| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2) & = & \sum_{\alpha, \beta} T^{(p)}_{\tau} (F_j (v_{\alpha}))^{\ast} T^{(p)}_{\tau} (F_j (v_{\beta})) \omega_j \left( \delta X_j (v_{\alpha})^{\ast} [\delta X_j (v_{\beta})]_{\frac{1}{p} + 2 \tau} \right)\\ \, & = & c'_{0, \frac{1}{p} + 2 \tau} | T^{(p)}_{\tau} (F_j) |^2_A \delta t_j \end{array}\)

compare with (4.8). Thus, instead of (4.9) we obtain the estimate

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (Y_t) \|_{h^p_c} \lesssim \left\| \left( \sum_{j \geqslant 0} | T^{(p)}_{\tau} (F_j ) |_A^2 \delta t_j \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_p,\)

and likewise for the \(\| \cdot \|_{h^p_r}\) norm. To control the diagonal Hardy norm, we use the additional assumption \(\| [X_{s, t} (f)]_r \| \lesssim_{r, f} | t - s |^{1 / 2}\) and obtain

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\tau} \left( \sum_j \| \delta Y_j \|^p_p \right)^{\frac{1}{p}} \lesssim \left( \sum_j \left( \sum_{\alpha} C_{\alpha} \| F_j (v_{\alpha}) \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \right)^p \delta t_j^{\frac{p}{2}} \right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\)

which for \(p > 2\) and finite-dimensional processes can be made arbitrary small by making the partition of \([0, t]\) finer. The conclusion holds by the density of \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\) in \(\mathbb{H}^p_A\), see Remark 4.14.\(\Box\)

Remark 4.16. If one requires weaker assumptions, e.g.

\(\displaystyle \| X_{s, t} (f) \|_{\mathbb{L}^q} \lesssim (t - s)^{\frac{1}{2}} \| A^{1 / 2} f \|_{\mathfrak{h}}, \qquad \forall 0 \leqslant s \leqslant t, \quad \forall f \in \mathfrak{h}, \) (4.13)

then one cannot obtain the result of Theorem 4.15 without modifying the topology on \(\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}^p\) in a base dependent way. Indeed, if we suppose that \(F \in \mathbb{H}^p_A\) is such that there is a basis \(\{ v_{\alpha} \}\) of \(\mathfrak{h}\) for which, for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\) and for \(F^{\#} = F, F^{\ast}\), we have

\(\displaystyle \left\| \left( \int_0^t \left| \sum_{\alpha} F_s^{\#} (v_{\alpha}) \| A^{1 / 2} v_{\alpha} \|_{\mathfrak{h}} \right|^2 \mathrm{d} s \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^r} < \infty, \) (4.14)

then the integral \(Y_t = \int_0^t \langle F_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle\) is well-defined. Indeed, for any \(F \in \mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\), we get

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega_j^{\left( \frac{r}{2} \right)} (| T^{(r)}_{\tau} (\delta Y_{j + 1}) |^2) & = & \sum_{\alpha, \beta} T^{(r)}_{\tau} (F_j (v_{\alpha}))^{\ast} T^{(r)}_{\tau} (F_j (v_{\beta})) \omega_j \left( \delta X_j (v_{\alpha})^{\ast} [\delta X_j (v_{\beta})]_{\frac{1}{r} + 2 \tau} \right)\\ \, & \leqslant & \delta t_j \left| \sum_{\alpha} T^{(r)}_{\tau} (F_j (v_{\alpha})) \| A^{1 / 2} v_{\alpha} \|_{\mathfrak{h}} \right|^2, \end{array}\)

from which one can conclude along the lines of the proof of Theorem 4.15.

4.1.3Itô–Grassmann integral

Now we want to focus on processes belonging to a Grassmann algebra (see Definition 3.7) generated by some analytic Grassmann Brownian motion \(X\) and an independent and compatible Grassmann algebra \(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0\), see Definition 3.9 and Lemma 3.10.

Definition 4.17. Consider the filtered modular space \((\mathcal{M}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t)_t)\), and let \(X : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{M}_a\) be an analytic GBM, and suppose that\(\mathcal{M}_0\) contains a Grassmann algebra \(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0\) of analytic elements which are independent of and compatible with the Grassmann algebra generated by \(X\). Then we call the space

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{G}_X := \operatorname{span} \left\{ a_0 X_{t_1} (v_1) \cdots X_{t_n} (v_n), \text{ where } a_0 \in \tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0, \; n \in \mathbb{N}_0, \; v_1, \ldots, v_n \in \mathfrak{h}, \; t_1, \ldots, t_n \in \mathbb{R}_+ \right\},\)

the Grassmann algebra generated by (\(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0\) and) \(X\). For every \(p \in [1, \infty]\) we let \(\mathcal{G}^p_X\) be the closure of \(\mathcal{G}_X\) with respect to the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) topology.

Remark 4.18. The algebra \(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0\) will be needed to study SDE with non-trivial initial conditions, see Sections 4.4 and 5.2.

The space \(\mathcal{G}_X\) is a Grassmann algebra, and thus we can define the set of even elements \(\mathcal{G}_{X, +}\) and odd elements \(\mathcal{G}_{X, -}\) and we have \(\mathcal{G}_X = \mathcal{G}_{X, +} \oplus \mathcal{G}_{X, -}\) , see Remark 3.8. Likewise, we can write \(\mathcal{G}^p_X = \mathcal{G}^p_{X, +} \oplus \mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) where \(\mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\)and \(\mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) are the closure of \(\mathcal{G}_{X, +}\) and \(\mathcal{G}_{X, -}\) with respect the topology of \(\mathbb{L}^p\). If an adapted process takes values in \(\mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\) or \(\mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) for some \(p \in [1, \infty]\), we will henceforth say that it is an even or odd process respectively. In particular we have that, for any \(v \in \mathfrak{h}\), \(X (v)\) is an odd process. We have the following fundamental result.

Proposition 4.19. Let \(X\) be an analytic GBM with covariance \(G_t = tG\) and let \(F \in \mathbb{H}^p_{| G | ; \operatorname{loc}}\) be an adapted process taking values in \(\mathcal{G}^p_X\). Then, for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), \(\int_0^t \langle F_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle \in \mathcal{G}^p_X\). Furthermore if \(F_s\) takes values in \(\mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\) then \(\int_0^t \langle F_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle_{\mathcal{}} \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) and if \(F_s\) takes values in \(\mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) then \(\int_0^t \langle F_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\).

Proof. The thesis is obvious in the case where \(F_s\) is a finite-dimensional simple process. The generic case can be obtained by the density of finite-dimensional simple processes in \(\mathbb{H}^p_{| G | ; \operatorname{loc}}\) and the continuity of the Itô integral in \(\mathbb{L}^p\) (an thus in \(\mathcal{G}^p_X\)).\(\Box\)

We conclude this section by introducing the notion of Itô processes with respect to a GBM, which, as shown in Lemma 3.18 satisfy the assumption of Theorem 4.15.

Definition 4.20. (Itô process). Let \(X\) be an analytic GBM with covariance \(G_t = tG\). We say that \(Y \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\) is a Itô process (of integrability \(p \geqslant 2\)) if \(Y_0 \in \mathcal{G}^p_X\), \(H \in \mathbb{H}^p_{| G | ; \operatorname{loc}} \cap \mathcal{G}^p_X\), and \(K : \mathbb{R}_+ \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\), \(\| K \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+)\) such that

\(\displaystyle Y_t = Y_0 + \int_0^t \langle H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \int_0^t K_s \mathrm{d} s \) (4.15)

where the second integral is in the Bochner sense. If \(H_s \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\), \(K_s \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) (resp. \(H_s \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\), \(K_s \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\)) we call \(Y\) an odd (resp. even) Itô process.

Remark 4.21. The above definition can be extended to the case of a multidimensional process \(X\) indexed in (pre-)Hilbert space \(W\). Indeed, we call the map \(Y_{\cdot} : \mathbb{R}_+ \times W \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\) an Itô process indexed in \(W\) and of integrability \(p \geqslant 2\)), a map that for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), \(Y_t (\cdot)\) is a linear operator, for any \(w \in W\), \(Y_{\cdot} (w) \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\), and there are \(H : \mathbb{R}_+ \times W \times K \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\) and \(K : \mathbb{R}_+ \times W \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\), which are linear in the \(W\) variable, and for any \(w \in W\) we have \(H_{\cdot} (w, \cdot) \in \mathbb{H}^p_{| G | ; \operatorname{loc}} \cap \mathcal{G}^p_X\), \(\| K_{\cdot} (w) \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \in L^1_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+)\), for which for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\) and \(w \in W\) we have

\(\displaystyle Y_t (w) = Y_0 (w) + \int_0^t \langle H_s (w, \cdot), \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \int_0^t K_s (w) \mathrm{d} s .\) (4.16)

If, for any \(s \in \mathbb{R}_+\), \(v \in \mathfrak{h}\) and \(w \in W\), we have \(F_s (w, v) \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\), \(K_s (w) \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) (resp. \(F_s \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\), \(K_s \in \mathcal{G}^p_{X, +}\)) we call \(Y\) an odd (resp. even) Itô random field.

Remark 4.22. The fact that \(Y \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\) or \(Y (w) \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\) follows directly from the representations (4.15) and (4.16). Furthermore, by Proposition 4.19, an odd/even Itô process is made (for fixed \(s \in \mathbb{R}_+\) and \(w \in W\)) by odd/even elements of \(\mathcal{G}^p_X\).

Remark 4.23. If \(Y_t\) is an Itô process in \(\mathbb{L}^p\) then, as consequence of Theorem 4.15, for every \(T > 0\), we have

\(\displaystyle \lim_{\pi \in \{ \text{partitions of } [0, T] \}, | \pi | \rightarrow 0} \left( \sup_{t_i \in \pi} \quad \sup_{t \in [t_i, t_{i - 1}]} \| Y_{t_{i - 1}} - Y_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 p}} \right) = 0\)

where the limit is taken with respect to the diameter \(| \pi |\) of partitions \(\pi\) going to \(0\). This property is analogous to the uniform continuity in \(L^p\) for commutative stochastic processes.

4.2Itô formula

In this section we want to prove an Itô formula for Itô processes in the sense of Definition 4.20. We remark that the following discussion could be extended to the anticommutative analogous of continuous commutative martingales - see, e.g., [RW00, Chapter IV, Section 5] for the latter notion.

Definition 4.24. Let \(p, q \in [1, \infty]\) and let \(B, B'\) be two martingales in \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{L}^p)\) and \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{L}^q)\) respectively. The quadratic variation \([B, B'] \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{L}^r)\) with \(1 / r = 1 / p + 1 / q\) is a process of bounded variation (in the \(\mathbb{L}^r\) norm) such that the process

\(\displaystyle t \mapsto B_t \cdot B'_t - [B, B']_t\)

is an martingale.

Lemma 4.25. Let \(X\) be an analytic GBM with covariance \(G_t = tG\). Then, for any \(v, v' \in \mathfrak{h}\) we have

\(\displaystyle [X (v), X (v')]_t = \langle \Theta v, G v' \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} t.\)

Proof. The proof is a consequence of the fact that the processes \(X_t (v)\) and \(X_t (v')\) have independent increments and so \(\omega_s ((X_{s, t}) (v) (X_{s, t}) (v')) = \omega_s ((X_{s, t}) (v) (X_{s, t}) (v')) = \langle \Theta v, G v' \rangle (t - s)\), for any \(s \leqslant t \in \mathbb{R}_+\).\(\Box\)

Theorem 4.26. Let \(Y_t, Y'_t\) be two Itô processes of integrability \(p \geqslant 2\) of the form

\(\displaystyle Y_t = Y_0 + \int_0^t \langle H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle, \quad Y'_t = Y_0' + \int_0^t \langle H_s', \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle,\)

where \(X\) is an analytic GBM indexed in \(\mathfrak{h} \)with covariance \(G_t = tG\). Then, we have

\(\displaystyle [Y, Y']_t = \int_0^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H'_s) \mathrm{d} s, \) (4.17)

where \(\operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (\cdot)\) is defined in Notation 4.11, \(\Theta\) being the conjugation in \(\mathfrak{h}\).

Proof. We prove the theorem in the special case of \(Y, Y'\) being simple finite-dimensional processes. The general case follows from the linearity of the quadratic variation and by density. On the other hand, formula (4.17) follows directly from the definition of simple processes, the properties of conditional expectation and Lemma 4.25.\(\Box\)

It will be convenient to work in some subspaces of the spaces \(\mathbb{H}^p_A\), see Definition 4.13.

Definition 4.27. Let \(F\) an \(\mathbb{L}^p\) adapted process indexed in a Hilbert space \(\mathfrak{h}\) with conjugation \(\Theta\) and let \(A \geqslant 0\) be a linear bounded operator on \(\mathfrak{h}\) commuting with \(\Theta\). We let

\(\displaystyle \| F_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p, A} := \| | F_s |_A \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}, \) (4.18)

and introduce the following subspace of \(\mathbb{H}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}}\)

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}} := \left\{ F \in \mathbb{H}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}} \, | \, \| F_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p, A} \in L^2_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+) \right\} .\)

Remark 4.28. One can check that \(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}}\) is indeed a subspace of \(\mathbb{H}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}}\), in fact

\(\displaystyle \| F \|_{\mathbb{H}^p_A ([0, t])} \lesssim \left( \int_0^t \| F_s \|^{2 p}_{\mathbb{L}^p, A} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} .\)

\(\)We also note that \(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}}\) are semimetric spaces with the set of semidistances

\(\displaystyle d_t (F, G) \mapsto \left( \int_0^t \| F_s - G_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p, A}^{2 p} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} .\)

Furthermore the finite-dimensional processes taking values in \(\mathbb{L}^p\) defined in Definition 4.9 are dense in \(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{A, \operatorname{loc}}\) with respect to its natural topology, compare with Remark 4.14.

Corollary 4.29. Under the hypothesis of Theorem 4.26, the following inequality holds

\(\displaystyle \int_0^t \| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H'_s) \|_{\mathbb{L}^r} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \| H \|_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{| G |} ([0, t])}^2 + \| H' \|_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^q_{| G |} ([0, t])}^2 . \) (4.19)

Proof. We will use the representation (4.17). Consider first \(H \in \mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\) and \(H \in \mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{ad}}\) and let \([s, t] \subset \mathbb{R}_+\) an interval where both \(H\) and \(H'\) are constant so that \(H_r \cdot H_{r'} \in \mathcal{M}_a \cap \mathcal{M}_s\) for \(r \in [s, t]\). Then, by Theorem 4.26 and by using that \(t \mapsto Y_t \cdot Y_t' - [Y, Y']_t\) is a martingale

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \int_s^t \| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_r \otimes H_r') \|_{\mathbb{L}^r} \mathrm{d} r & = & \left\| \omega_s \left( \int_s^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_r \otimes H_r') \mathrm{d} r \right) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^r}\\ & = & \| \omega_s ([Y, Y']_t - [Y, Y']_s) \|_{\mathbb{L}^r}\\ & = & \left\| \omega_s \left( \int_s^t \langle H_r, \mathrm{d} X_r \rangle \right) \left( \int_s^t \langle H_{r'}', \mathrm{d} X_{r'} \rangle \right) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^r} . \end{array}\)

By Hölder's inequality, Theorem 4.15 and the bound \(\| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{H}^p_A ([0, t])}^2 \leqslant \| \cdot \|^2_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_A ([0, t])}\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \int_s^t \| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_r \otimes H_r') \|_{\mathbb{L}^r} \mathrm{d} r & \leqslant & \left\| \left( \int_s^t \langle H_r, \mathrm{d} X_r \rangle \right) \left( \int_s^t \langle H_{r'}', \mathrm{d} X_{r'} \rangle \right) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^r}\\ & \lesssim & \left( \int_s^t \| H_r \|^2_{\mathbb{L}^p, | G |} \mathrm{d} r \right) + \left( \int_s^t \| H_s' \|^2_{\mathbb{L}^q, | G |} \mathrm{d} r \right), \end{array}\)

Taking the sum over a partition where both \(H_s\) and \(H'_s\) are constant, we obtain (4.19).\(\Box\)

We introduce the following standard notation.

Notation 4.30. If \(Y, Y'\) are Itô processes of the form \(Y_t = Y_0 + \int_0^t \left\langle {H_s} , \mathrm{d} X_s \right\rangle + \int_0^t K_s \mathrm{d} s\), \(Y_t' = Y_0' + \int_0^t \left\langle {H_s'} , \mathrm{d} X_s \right\rangle + \int_0^t K_s' \mathrm{d} s\), and \(\tilde{H}\) and \(\tilde{K}\) are adapted processes, we use the notation

\(\displaystyle \int_0^t \tilde{H}_s \mathrm{d} Y_s := \int_0^t \langle \tilde{H}_s H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \int_0^t \tilde{H}_s K_s \mathrm{d} s, \qquad \int_0^t \tilde{K}_s \mathrm{d} [Y, Y']_s := \int_0^t \tilde{K}_s \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H'_s) \mathrm{d} s.\)

We want to prove an Itô formula for Itô processes indexed in some finite-dimensional linear space \(W\). To this end, we introduce the space of polynomials of degree at most \(k \in \mathbb{N}\) of commutative and anticommutative random variables indexed in \(W\):

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{F}^k (W) = \bigoplus_{n = 0}^k \bigoplus_{\ell = 0}^n \Lambda^{n - \ell} W \otimes \odot^{\ell} W,\)

i.e. the tensor product of the antisymmetric and symmetric vector spaces generated by \(W\). We also use the notation \(\mathcal{F}^{\infty} (W) = \bigcup_{k \in \mathbb{N}} \mathcal{F}^k (W)\). The space \(\mathcal{F}^{\infty} (W)\) is an algebra with the following multiplication: if \(F \in \mathcal{F}^k (W)\), that is,

\(\displaystyle F = \sum_{n = 0}^k \sum_{\ell = 0}^n F^{(n, \ell)} \otimes \hat{F}^{(n, \ell)}, \) (4.20)

where \(F^{(n, \ell)} \in \Lambda^{n - \ell} W\) and \(\hat{F}^{(n, \ell)} \in \bigodot^{\ell} W\), for any \(G \in \Lambda^m W\) and \(G' \in \bigodot^{m'} W\) we set

\(\displaystyle (G \otimes G') \cdot F := \sum_{n = 0}^k \sum_{\ell = 0}^n (G \wedge F^{(n, \ell)}) \otimes (G' \odot \hat{F}^{(n, \ell)}) . \) (4.21)

If \(Z_- : W \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_{X, -}\) is an odd Grassmann random field and \(F = \sum_i w_{\alpha_i^1} \wedge \cdots \wedge w_{\alpha_i^k} \in \Lambda^k W\), \(n \leqslant p\), where \(w_{\alpha_i^n} \in W\), we can define

\(\displaystyle F (Z_-) = \sum_i Z_- (w_{\alpha^1_i}) \cdots Z_- (w_{\alpha^k_i})\)

which is a well-defined object (not depending on the explicit representation of \(F\)) since \(Z\) is odd. In the same way if \(Z_+\) is even and \(F = \sum_i w_{\alpha_i^1} \odot \cdots \odot w_{\alpha_i^k} \in \bigodot^k W\) we can define \(F (Z_-) = \sum_i Z_+ (w_{\alpha_i^1}) \cdots Z_+ (w_{\alpha_i^k})\). In this way if \(Z : W \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\) is a generic random field, which can be decomposed in a unique way \(Z = Z_- + Z_+\), where \(Z_-, Z_+\) are even and odd respectively, and considering a generic element \(F \in \mathcal{F}^k (W)\), see (4.20), we define

\(\displaystyle F (Z) := \sum_{n = 0}^k \sum_{\ell = 0}^n F^{(n, \ell)} (Z_-) \cdot \hat{F}^{(n, \ell)} (Z_+) . \) (4.22)

With the previous notation we have that if \(F, G \in \mathcal{F}^{\infty} (W)\) and \(Z\) has enough integrability we have \((F \cdot G) (Z) = F (Z) \cdot G (Z)\) (where the first product is the product of polynomials in the sense of definition (4.21), and the second is the product in the twisted space \(\mathbb{L}^p\)).

We introduce commutative and anticommutative derivatives on \(\mathcal{F}^k (W)\).

Definition 4.31. For any \(w \in W\), we define two linear maps \(\partial^a_w : \mathcal{F}^k (W) \rightarrow \mathcal{F}^{k - 1} (W)\) and \(\partial^c_w : \mathcal{F}^k (W) \rightarrow \mathcal{F}^{k - 1} (W)\) by the following recursive relation, for any \(v \in W\),

\(\displaystyle \partial^a_w (1 \otimes v) = \partial^c_w (v \otimes 1) = 0,\)

\(\displaystyle \partial^a_w ((v \otimes 1) \cdot F) = \langle w, v \rangle_W F - (w \otimes 1) \cdot \partial^a_w F, \qquad \partial^c_w ((1 \otimes v) \cdot F) = \langle w, v \rangle_h F + (1 \otimes w) \cdot \partial^a_w F.\)

This allows us to provide a general Taylor formula for polynomials in \(\mathcal{F}^k (W)\).

Lemma 4.32. Consider \(F \in \mathcal{F}^k (W)\), let \(Z = Z_- + Z_+, R = R_- + R_+ : W \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\) be random fields indexed in \(h\) (where \(Z_-, R_-\) and \(Z_+, R_+\) are respectively even and odd ), and let \(\{ w_j \}_{j = 1}^N\) be an orthonormal basis of \(W\), then

\begin{eqnarray*} F (Z) - F (R) & = & \sum_{n = 0}^k \sum_{\ell = 0}^n \sum_{j_1, \ldots, j_{n - \ell}, j_1', \ldots, j_{\ell}' = 1}^N \frac{1}{(n - \ell) ! \ell !} \partial^a_{w_{j_1}} \partial^a_{w_{j_2}} \cdots \partial^a_{w_{j_{n - \ell}}} \partial^c_{w_{j_1'}} \cdots \partial^c_{w_{j_{\ell}'}} (F) (R) \cdot\\ & & \hspace{7em} \cdot \left( \prod_{o = 1}^{n - \ell} (Z_- (w_{j_o}) - R_- (w_{j_o})) \right) \cdot \left( \prod_{o = 1}^{\ell} (Z_+ (w_{j_o'}) - R_+ (w_{j_o'})) \right) . \end{eqnarray*}

We omit the proof, it being a simple generalisation of the usual Taylor formula. We are now ready to prove an Itô formula for polynomials of Itô processes.

Theorem 4.33. Let \(\mathfrak{h}\) be a Hilbert space with conjugation \(\Theta\). Let \(X\) is an analytic GBM indexed in \(\mathfrak{h}\) with covariance \(G_t = tG\). Consider \(Y_t = Y_{t, -} + Y_{t, +}\) be a generic Itô process of integrability \(p \geqslant 2\), indexed in a finite-dimensional vector space \(W\), where \(Y_{t, -}\) and \(Y_{t, +}\) are an odd and even Itô random fields such that

\(\displaystyle Y_{\rho} = Y_{0, \rho} + \int_0^t K_{s, \rho} (\cdot) \mathrm{d} s + \int_0^t \langle H_{s, \rho} (\cdot, \cdot), \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle, \qquad \rho \mathop{=}\limits \pm\)

where \(K_-, K_+ : W \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\) are odd and even processes respectively and \(H_{s, -} (w, \cdot), H_{s, +} (w, \cdot) \in \mathbb{H}^p_{| G |, \operatorname{loc}}\) are even and odd processes respectively. Consider \(F \in \mathcal{F}^n (W)\), where \(n \leqslant \frac{p}{2}\), then, for any orthonormal basis \(\{ v_j \}_{\alpha = 1}^N\) of \(W\), we have

\(\displaystyle F (Y_t) - F (Y_0)\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{\alpha} \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^a F (Y_s) \mathrm{d} Y^{\alpha}_{s, -} + \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^c F (Y_s) \mathrm{d} Y^{\alpha}_{s, +} + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^a \partial^a_{v_{\beta}} F (Y_s) \mathrm{d} [Y^{\alpha}_-, Y^{\beta}_-]_s\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^c \partial^c_{v_{\beta}} F (Y_s) \mathrm{d} [Y^{\alpha}_+, Y_+^{\beta}]_s + \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^c \partial^a_{v_{\beta}} F (Y_s) \mathrm{d} [Y^{\alpha}_+, Y^{\beta}_-]_s\) (4.23)

where \(Y^{\alpha}_{\rho} = Y_{\rho} (v_{\alpha}),\) \(\rho = \pm\), and the integrals are defined in function of \(X\), \(K_-\), \(K_+\), \(H_-\) and \(H_+\) as in Notation 4.30.

Remark 4.34. If \(Y\) is odd and \(F \in \bigoplus_{n = 0}^k \Lambda^n W\) the composition \(F (Y_t)\) is well-defined (as a particular case of equation (4.22)) and Theorem 4.33 implies

\(\displaystyle F (Y_t) - F (Y_0) = \sum_{\alpha} \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^a F (Y_s) \mathrm{d} Y^{\alpha}_s + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^a \partial^a_{v_{\beta}} F (Y_s) \mathrm{d} [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_s, \) (4.24)

which is the perfect analogous of the Itô formula in standard stochastic calculus. If \(F\) is even and \(F \in \bigoplus_{n = 0}^k \bigodot^n W\) Itô formula (4.24) holds with \(\partial_{v_{\alpha}}^c\) in place of \(\partial_{v_{\beta}}^a\).

Proof of Theorem 4.33. The proof is along the lines of the standard proof of the Itô formula in the commutative setting (see, e.g., [Le 16, Section 5.2]). For this reason, we sketch it in the special case \(K_- = K_+ = H_+ = 0\). Furthermore, we consider the convergence in \(\mathbb{L}^2\) since \(\mathbb{L}^p \subset \mathbb{L}^2\) for any \(p > 2\); in fact, by proving the equality in \(\mathbb{L}^2\), because both members belong to \(\mathbb{L}^p\) we have that the equality holds in \(\mathbb{L}^p\) as well.

We write \(Y^{\alpha}_t = Y_{t, -} (v_{\alpha})\), and \(H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot) = H_{s, -} (v_{\alpha}, \cdot)\). Furthermore, since in the proof only anticommutative derivatives \(\partial^a\) are needed, we use the notation by \(\partial_{v_{\alpha}} \equiv \partial_{v_{\alpha}}^a\), \(\partial_{v_{\alpha_1} \ldots v_{\alpha_i}}^i \equiv \partial_{v_{\alpha_1}}^a \cdots \partial_{v_{\alpha_i}}^a\). Being \(F\) a polynomial of degree at most \(n\), for any \(\alpha_1, \ldots {,} \alpha_i\) we have

\(\displaystyle \left\| \partial_{v_{\alpha_1} \ldots v_{\alpha_i}}^i F (Y_t) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n / (n - i)}} \lesssim (1 + \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}})^{n - i}, \) (4.25)

where the constant in the symbol \(\lesssim\) depends only on \(F\). Furthermore, by Lemma 4.32, we get

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} F (Y_t) - F (Y_0) = \sum_{t_i \in \pi} F (Y_{t_i}) - F (Y_{t_{i - 1}})\\ \qquad = \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \sum_{\alpha} \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}})\\ \hspace{7em} + \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \sum_{i = 3}^n \frac{1}{i!} \sum_{\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_i = 1}^k \partial_{v_{\alpha_1} \ldots v_{a_i}}^i F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha_1}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha_1}_{t_{i - 1}}) \cdots (Y^{\alpha_i}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha_i}_{t_{i - 1}}) . \end{array} \) (4.26)

Furthermore, combining (4.25) and (4.26), we get

\(\displaystyle \left\| \partial_{v_{\alpha_1} \ldots v_{\alpha_i}}^i F (Y_t) - \partial_{v_{\alpha_1} \ldots v_{\alpha_i}}^i F (Y_s) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n / (n - i)}} \lesssim (1 + \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}} + \| Y_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}})^{n - i} \| Y_t - Y_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}} . \) (4.27)

Let us focus on each term in the sum in (4.26). For any \(| \tau | \leqslant \frac{3}{4}\), we have that

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \left\| T^{(2)}_{\tau} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) - \int_0^t \langle \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_s) H_{s, -}, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle \right) \right\|_{L^2}^2\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \left\| T^{(2)}_{\tau} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \left( \int_{t_{i - 1}}^{t_i} \langle (\partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) - \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_s)) H_{s, -}, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle \right) \right) \right\|^2_{L^2}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \int_{t_{i - 1}}^{t_i} \left\| T^{\left( \frac{2 n}{n - 1} \right)}_{\tau_1} (\partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) - \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_s)) \cdot \right. \)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \hspace{3em} \left. \cdot \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (T^{(2 n)}_{\tau_2} (H_s) \otimes T^{(2 n)}_{\tau_2} (H^{\ast}_s)) T^{\left( \frac{2 n}{n - 1} \right)}_{\tau_1} (\partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) - \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_s)) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^1} \mathrm{d} s\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \sup_{s \in [t_i, t_{i - 1}]} \| \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) - \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_s) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n / (n - 1)}}^2 \left( \sum_{\alpha} \int_{t_{i - 1}}^{t_i} \| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s^{\alpha} \otimes H^{\alpha \ast}_s) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}} \mathrm{d} s \right)\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \left( \sum_{\alpha} \int_0^t \| H_s^{\alpha} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p, | G |} \mathrm{d} s \right) (\sup_{s \in [0, t]} (1 + \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}))^{2 n - 4} (\max_{t_i \in \pi} \sup_{s \in [t_i, t_{i - 1}]} \| Y_{t_{i - 1}} - Y_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}^2),\) (4.28)

where \(\tau_1, \tau_2 \geqslant 0\) are suitable real numbers, and in the last step we used the inequalities (4.19) and (4.27).

By the inequality (4.27) and by Remark 4.23 applied to the Itô process \(Y_t\), the last term goes to zero. Taking the sup over \(| \tau | \leqslant \frac{3}{4}\) in (4.28), we obtain that \(\sum_{t_i \in \pi} \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}})\) converges to \(\int_0^t \langle \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_s) H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot), \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle\) as \(| \pi | \rightarrow 0\) in \(\mathbb{L}^2\).

By using the fact that \(Y_s^{\alpha} Y_s^{\beta} - [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_s\) is a martingale, see Definition 4.24, we get that, for any \(| \tau | \leqslant \frac{3}{4}\),

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \left\| T^{(2)}_{\tau} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) \left( (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) \left( Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}} \right) - [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_i} + [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_{i - 1}} \right) \right) \right\|_{L^2}^2 =\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \left. \left\| T^{(n)}_{\tau_1} \left( (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}}) - \left[ Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta} \right]_{t_i} + [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_{i - 1}} \right) T^{\left( \frac{2 n}{n - 2} \right)}_{\tau_2} (\partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}})) \cdot \right. \right.\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \left. \cdot T^{\left( \frac{2 n}{n - 1} \right)}_{\tau_2} (\partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}))^{\ast} T^{(n)}_{\tau_2} \left( (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}}) - [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_i} + [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_{i - 1}} \right)^{\ast} \right\|_{L^1}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \| (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}}) - [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_i} + [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_{i - 1}} \|_{\mathbb{L}^n}^2 \| \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n / (n - 2)}}^2\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle (\sup_{s \in [0, t]} (1 + \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}}))^{2 n - 4} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \| (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}}) \|_{\mathbb{L}^n}^2 + \| [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_i} - [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_{i - 1}} \|^2_{\mathbb{L}^n} \right)\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle (\sup_{s \in [0, t]} (1 + \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}))^{2 n - 4} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} (\| H^{\alpha} \|^2_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^n_{| G |} ([t_{i - 1}, t_i])} + \| H^{\beta} \|^2_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^n_{| G |} ([t_{i - 1}, t_i])})^2 \right)\) (4.29)

where we used that \(n \leqslant p / 2\), which goes to zero as \(| \pi | \rightarrow 0\) since the map \(t \mapsto \| H^{\alpha} \|^2_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{| G |} ([0, t])}\) (and thus the map \(t \mapsto \| H^{\alpha} \|^2_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^n_{| G |} ([0, t])}\))\(\) is an absolutely continuous function. On the other hand, we have that

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \left\| T^{(2)}_{\tau} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) \left( [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_i} - [Y^{\alpha}, Y^{\beta}]_{t_{i - 1}} \right) - \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_s) \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot) \otimes H_s^{\beta} (\cdot)) \mathrm{d} s \right) \right\|_{L^2}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \left\| T^{(2)}_{\tau} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \int_{t_{i - 1}}^{t_i} (\partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) - \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_s)) \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot) \otimes H_s^{\beta} (\cdot)) \mathrm{d} s \right) \right\|_{L^2}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \int_{t_{i - 1}}^{t_i} \| (\partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) - \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_s)) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n / (n - 2)}} \| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot) \otimes H^{\beta}_s (\cdot)) \|_{\mathbb{L}^n} \mathrm{d} s\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle (\sup_{s \in [0, t]} (1 + \| Y_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 p}}))^{n - 3} (\sup_{t_i \in \pi} \sup_{t \in [t_i, t_{i - 1}]} \| Y_{t_{i - 1}} - Y_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 p}}) (\| H^{\alpha} (\cdot) \|^2_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}_{| G |}^p ([0, t])} + \| H^{\beta} (\cdot) \|^2_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^p_{| G |} ([0, t])}) \) (4.30)

which converges to \(0\) by Remark 4.23 applied to the Itô process \(Y_t\). Thus, taking the supremum over \(| \tau | \leqslant \frac{3}{4}\), the inequalities (4.29) and (4.29) imply that \(\frac{1}{2} \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \partial_{v_{\alpha} v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}})\) converges to \(\int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}, v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_s) \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot) \otimes H^{\beta}_s (\cdot)) \mathrm{d} s\) in \(\mathbb{L}^2\). Finally we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \left\| \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \partial_{v_{\alpha_1} \ldots v_{a_k}}^k F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha_1}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha_1}_{t_{i - 1}}) \cdots (Y^{\alpha_i}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha_i}_{t_{i - 1}}) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^2}\\ \quad \leqslant \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \left\| \partial_{v_{\alpha_1} \ldots v_{a_k}}^k F (Y_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\alpha_1}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha_1}_{t_{i - 1}}) \cdots (Y^{\alpha_i}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha_i}_{t_{i - 1}}) \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^2}\\ \quad \lesssim \sup_{s \in [0, t]} (1 + \| Y_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p})^{n - k} \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \| Y_{t_i} - Y_{t_{i - 1}} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}^k\\ \quad \lesssim \sup_{s \in [0, t]} (1 + \| Y_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^p})^{n - k} \sum_{\alpha} \left( \sum_{t_i \in \pi} \left( \int_{t_{i - 1}}^{t_i} \operatorname{Tr}_{| G |} (H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot) \otimes H_s^{\alpha \ast} (\cdot)) \mathrm{d} s \right)^{\frac{k}{2}} \right) . \end{array}\)

The last term converges to \(0\) since \(k > 2\). This means that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} F (Y_t) - F (Y_0) = \lim_{| \pi | \rightarrow 0} \sum_{t_i \in \pi} F (Y_{t_i}) - F (Y_{t_{i - 1}})\\ \quad = \sum_{\alpha} \int_0^t \langle \partial_{v_{\alpha}} F (Y_s) H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot), \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\alpha, \beta} \int_0^t \partial_{v_{\alpha}, v_{\beta}}^2 F (Y_s) \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s^{\alpha} (\cdot) \otimes H_s^{\beta} (\cdot)) \mathrm{d} s. \end{array}\)
\(\Box\)

Remark 4.35. The fact that the Itô process \(Y\) takes values in a Grassmann algebra (namely \(\mathcal{G}^p_X\)) is used in a essential way in the proof for obtaining the last three terms in the Itô formula (4.23), namely the terms involving the quadratic variation. Indeed in order to get the quadratic variation, we need to pull through the term \((Y^{\alpha}_{t_i} - Y^{\alpha}_{t_{i - 1}}) (Y^{\beta}_{t_i} - Y^{\beta}_{t_{i - 1}})\) in the Taylor expansion which is possible thanks to the anticommutativity or commutativity of the \(Y^{\alpha}\)'s.

Remark 4.36. An important consequence of Theorem 4.33 is that sums and products of Itô processes taking values in a Grassmann algebra are again an Itô processes. In other words, Itô processes are closed with respect to algebraic operations.

4.3Girsanov's formula

In this section we will prove a form of Girsanov's theorem involving Itô processes in the algebra \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X := \bigcap_{p \geqslant 1} \mathcal{G}^p_X\) (see Remark 4.40 below for more details). Since \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) is only a Fréchet algebra, but the notions of GBM and conditional expectation were so far discussed for modular spaces \((\mathcal{M}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t)_t)\), we shall now extend them. First of all, we introduce the notion of signed expectation (or state) for a generic Fréchet algebra \(\mathcal{A}\).

Definition 4.37. Let \(\mathcal{A}\) be a Fréchet algebra. We call a continuous linear functional \(\overline{\mathcal{E}} : \mathcal{A} \rightarrow \mathbb{C}\) a signed expectation (or state) on \(\mathcal{A}\) . Let \(\{ \mathcal{A}_t \}_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) be a filtration of Fréchet subalgebra of \(\mathcal{A}\) and let \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t : \mathcal{A} \rightarrow \mathcal{A}_t\) be a family of linear continuous operators, we say \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t\) is a conditional expectation associated with \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) if for any \(a \in \mathcal{A}\), \(t, s \in \mathbb{R}_+\), \(t \geqslant s \geqslant 0\), \(b, c \in \mathcal{A}_s\) we have

  1. \(\overline{\mathcal{E}} (\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t (a)) = \overline{\mathcal{E}} (a)\),

  2. \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_s (\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t (a)) = \overline{\mathcal{E}}_s (a)\),

  3. \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_s (b a c) = b \overline{\mathcal{E}}_s (a) c\).

Remark 4.38. Note that in a generic Fréchet algebra (without a \(^{\ast}\) operation) we do not have the notion of positive operator and thus of positive linear functional. Yet, in the particular case of a Fréchet \(^{\ast}\)-algebra the expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) is generally not a positive linear functional.

A process \(X \in \mathcal{A}\) is a martingale with respect to the signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) if, as usual,

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{E}_s} (X_t) = X_s, \qquad \forall s \leqslant t.\)

We can also define a concept of law for random fields with respect to a signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\): we say that two random fields \(B, B' : \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{A}\), defined on the same vector space \(\mathfrak{h}\), has the same law with respect to the signed state \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) if and only if the \(n\) points functions of the two fields are the same; i.e. for any \(v_1, \ldots, v_n \in \mathfrak{h}\) we have

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{E}} (B (v_1) \cdots B (v_n)) = \overline{\mathcal{E}} (B' (v_1) \cdots B' (v_n)) .\)

We can extend the same notion for processes, and thus we are able to define the notion of a Brownian motion with covariance \(G\) with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) and of martingale.

Definition 4.39. Let \(\mathcal{A}\) be a Fréchet algebra with signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) and let \(\mathcal{B} = \mathcal{B}_- \oplus \mathcal{B}_+ \subset \mathcal{A}\) be a Grassmann subalgebra of \(\mathcal{A}\). Let \(\mathfrak{h}\) be a separable Hilbert space with conjugation \(\Theta\). We say that a process \(X : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{B}_-\) is a weak Grassmann Brownian motion with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) and with covariance \(G\), if it is a martingale and if, for any \(v_1, \ldots, v_{2 n} \in \mathfrak{h}\) and \(t_1, \ldots, t_{2 n} \in \mathbb{R}_+\), we have

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{E}} \left( \prod_{i = 1}^{2 n} X_{t_i} (v_i) \right) = \sum_{\mathcal{M} \in \text{Perfect matches of } \{ 1, \ldots, 2 n \} } (- 1)^{\mathcal{M}} \prod_{(i, j) \in \mathcal{M}} \langle \Theta v_i, Gv_j \rangle (t_i \wedge t_j) .\)

Obviously the \(n\)-point functions of Brownian motion defined with respect a signed state \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) are equal to the ones of Brownian motion with respect to a positive state, as defined in Definition 3.14.

Remark 4.40. Hereafter we consider the following setting: we fix a filtered modular space \((\mathcal{M}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t)_t)\), for which there is an analytic Brownian motion \(X_t\), defined on \(\mathfrak{h}\), and the Grassmann algebra \(\mathcal{G}_X\) and \(\mathcal{G}^p_X\) generated by \(X\) and some compatible independent Grassmann algebra \(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0 \subset \mathcal{M}_0\) (see Definition 4.17). We then consider the space

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{G}}_X := \bigcap_{p \geqslant 1} \mathcal{G}^p_X, \) (4.31)

which is, thanks to Hölder's inequality, a Fréchet algebra with the seminorms \(\| \cdot \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}\). Furthermore \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) is Grassmann and the even and odds elements are \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, \pm} = \bigcap_{p \geqslant 1} \mathcal{G}^p_{X, \pm}\). We call the case where \(\mathcal{A} = \overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) the standard setting.

In Section 2.4 we extended the conditional expectation \(\omega_t\) to the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces, see Remark 2.23, so that the the state \(\omega\) is also a signed expectation with conditional expectation \(\omega_t\) on \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) in the sense of Definition 4.37. We shall now describe a standard way of building from \(\omega\) and \(\omega_t\) a signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) with a conditional expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t\) on the Fréchet algebra \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\).

Lemma 4.41. In the standard setting of Remark 4.40, consider a martingale \(\{ Z_t \}_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+} \subset \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, +}\) such that \(Z_0 = 1\), \(Z_{\infty} = \lim_{t \rightarrow + \infty} Z_t\) exists, \(\omega_t (Z_{\infty}) = Z_t\), and, for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\) there is \(Z_t^{- 1} \in \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, +}\) such that \(Z_t \cdot Z_t^{- 1} = 1\). Then, if for every \(a \in \overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) and \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), we define

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z (a) = \omega (a Z_{\infty}), \qquad \overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z (a) = \omega_t (aZ_{\infty}) Z_t^{- 1} . \) (4.32)

Then \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z\) is an signed expectation on \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) with associated conditional expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z\) with respect to the filtration \(\{ \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, t} \}_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+} := \{ \overline{\mathcal{G}}_X \cap \mathbb{L}^1 (\mathcal{M}_t) \}_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) of \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\), in the sense of Definition 4.37.

Proof. The continuity of \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z\) and \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z\) is given by Hölder's inequality for the twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces. Furthermore properties 1, 2, 3 of Definition 4.37 hold. Indeed, we have that, for any \(a \in \overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) and \(s \leqslant t\),

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{E}}_s^Z (\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z (a)) = \omega_s (\omega_t (a Z_{\infty}) Z_t^{- 1} Z_{\infty}) Z_s^{- 1} = \omega_s (\omega_t (a Z_{\infty}) Z_t^{- 1} \omega_t (Z_{\infty})) Z_s^{- 1} = \omega_s (\omega_t (a Z_{\infty})) Z_s^{- 1} = \overline{\mathcal{E}}_s^Z (a),\)

and similarly in the case where \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_s^Z\) is replaced by \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z\). Furthermore for any \(b, c \in \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, t}\) we get

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z (b a c) = \omega_t (b a c Z_{\infty}) Z_t^{- 1} = \omega_t (b a Z_{\infty} c) Z_t^{- 1} = b \omega_t (a Z_{\infty}) c Z_t^{- 1} = b \overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z_t (a) c\)

where we used that \(Z_{\infty}, Z_t^{- 1}\) commute with any element of \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) being both elements of \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, +}\).\(\Box\)

Below, it will be important to characterize weak Grassmann Brownian motion. To this end, we will use the following theorem, which is a non-commutative version of Lévy's characterization theorem.

Theorem 4.42. (Non-commutative Lévy's characterization). Suppose we are in the standard setting, see Remark 4.40, and let \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) be a signed state on \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) and admitting the conditional expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t\). Consider the Itô process \(B_t = \int_0^t \langle H'_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \int_0^t K_s \mathrm{d} s\), with \(\| H'_{\cdot} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p, | G |} \in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+)\), \(K \in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\), for every \(p \geqslant 2\), which is a martingale with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t\). Then, \(B\) is a weak Brownian motion with covariance \(G\) and with respect to the signed state \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) if and only if \(B_0 = 0\) and the quadratic variation \([B (v), B (v')]_t\) with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t\) is \([B (v), B (v')]_t = \langle \Theta v, Gv' \rangle t\).

The proof of Theorem 4.42 requires the following technical lemma.

Lemma 4.43.
