Let \(Y_t, Y'_t\) be two Itô processes taking values in \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) such that \(Y \in L^2_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^{2 p}_X)\) and \(Y_t' = \int_0^t \langle H'_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \int_0^t K_s' \mathrm{d} s\) with \(\| H'_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 q}, | G |} \in L^2_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+)\), \(K \in L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^{2 q}_X)\) and \(\frac{1}{p} + \frac{1}{q} = 1\). Then, for each \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), the following identity holds in \(\mathbb{L}^2\)

\(\displaystyle \lim_{| \pi | \rightarrow 0} \sum_{t_i \in \pi} Y_{t_{i - 1} \wedge t} (Y_{t_i \wedge t}' - Y_{t_{i - 1} \wedge t}') = \int_0^t Y_s \mathrm{d} Y'_s .\)

Proof. The proof is similar to the one of Theorem 4.33 (see in particular equation (4.33)).\(\Box\)

An important consequence of Lemma 4.43 is the following.

Corollary 4.44. Let \(Y_t, Y'_t\)be two Itô processes satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma 4.43. Then, if \(Y'_t\) is a martingale with respect to the signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\), also \(\int_0^t Y_s \mathrm{d} Y'_s\) is a martingale with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\). Furthermore, if \(U, U'\) is an other pair of Itô processes satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma 4.43, such that \(U'_t = \int_0^t \langle T_s', \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \int_0^t L_s \mathrm{d} s\) is a martingale with respect to the signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\), then the quadratic variation of \(\int_0^t Y_s \mathrm{d} Y'_s\) and \(\int_0^t U_s \mathrm{d} U_s'\) with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) is

\(\displaystyle \left[ \int_0^{\cdot} Y_s \mathrm{d} Y'_s, \int_0^{\cdot} W_s \mathrm{d} W'_s \right]_t = \int_0^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} ((Y_s \cdot H'_s) \otimes (U_s \cdot T_s')) \mathrm{d} s.\)

Proof. The proof of the first part is a consequence of the fact that \(\sum_{t_i \in \pi} Y_{t_{i - 1} \wedge t} (Y_{t_i \wedge t}' - Y_{t_{i - 1} \wedge t}')\) is a \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) martingale whenever \(Y'\) is a \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) martingale, and the fact that the martingale property is preserved by taking limits in \(\mathbb{L}^2\), \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t\) being continuous. The second part of the statement is a consequence of the first part and the Itô formula.\(\Box\)

Proof of Theorem 4.42. Let \(\theta_1, \ldots, \theta_n \in \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, -, 0}\) be independent and compatible random variables which are (non-zero) elements of \(\)\(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0\) (by Lemma 3.10 it is always possible to assume \(\)that \(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_0\) contains a numerable set of independent and compatible Grassmann random variables). Consider \(f_1, \ldots, f_n : \mathbb{R}_+ \rightarrow \mathfrak{h}\) smooth compactly supported functions. Let \(\mathcal{A} \subset \mathcal{N} = \{ 1, \ldots, n \}\) and define

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}} (t) = \overline{\mathcal{E}}_0 \left( \prod_{i \in \mathcal{A}} \left( 1 + \theta_i \int_0^t \langle f_i (s), \mathrm{d} B_s \rangle \right) \right) .\)

The functions \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}} (t)\) take value in the finite-dimensional Grassmann algebra \(\mathfrak{G} =\operatorname{span} \left\{ \prod_{j \in \mathcal{A}} \theta_j, \quad \mathcal{A} \subset \mathcal{N} \right\}\). Furthermore, the coefficients of the function \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}\) contain all the possible \(n\)-point functions of the Grassmann random variables \(\int_0^t \langle f_i (s), \mathrm{d} B_s \rangle\), indeed

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}} (t) = \sum_{\mathcal{B} \subset \mathcal{A}} \mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{B}} (t) \theta_{\mathcal{B}}\)

where \(\theta_{\mathcal{B}} = \prod_{\ell, \{ i_1 < \ldots < i_k \} = \mathcal{B}} \theta_{i_{\ell}}\) and

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{B}} (t) = (- 1)^{\mathcal{B}} \overline{\mathcal{E}} \left( \prod_{\ell, \{ i_1 < \ldots < i_k \} = \mathcal{B}} \int_0^t \langle f_{i_{\ell}} (s), \mathrm{d} B_s \rangle \right) .\)

This means that, if for any \(\mathcal{A} \subset \mathcal{N}\), the function \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}} (t)\) is equal to

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{A}} (t) = \mathcal{E}_0 \left( \prod_{i \in \mathcal{A}} \left( 1 + \theta_i \int_0^t \langle f_i (s), \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle \right) \right)\)

where \(X\) is some Brownian motion with covariance \(G\) with respect to \(\omega\), then \(B\) has the same law of a Brownian motion.

Suppose that \(B\) is a \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) martingale and that it has quadratic variation with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) equal to \(\langle G^{\ast} \Theta \cdot, \cdot \rangle t\). By Corollary 4.44 also \(\int_0^t \langle f_j (s) \mathrm{d} B_s \rangle\) are martingales with quadratic variation given by \(\int_0^t \langle G^{\ast} \Theta f_i (s), f_j (s) \rangle \mathrm{d} s\). This means that, if we apply the Itô formula to the product \(\prod_{i \in \mathcal{A}} \left( 1 + \theta_i \int_0^t \langle f_i (s), \mathrm{d} B_s \rangle \right)\), we get that \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}\) satisfies the following system of ODE

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}} (t) = 1 + \sum_{\{ i, j \} \subset \mathcal{A}} \int_0^t \langle G^{\ast} \Theta f_i (s), f_j (s) \rangle \theta_j \theta_i \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A} \backslash \{ i, j \}} (s) \mathrm{d} s.\)

Since the previous system of ODE for \(\{ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}} \}_{\mathcal{A} \subset \mathcal{N}}\) is takes value in the finite-dimensional (Grassmann) algebra \(\mathfrak{G}\) it has a unique solution. On the other hand, using again the Itô formula, the previous system of ODEs is satisfied by the \(n\)-point functions of the Brownian motion \(X\) with covariance \(G\), namely

\(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{A}} (t) = 1 + \sum_{\{ i, j \} \subset \mathcal{A}} \int_0^t \langle G^{\ast} \Theta f_i (s), f_j (s) \rangle \theta_j \theta_i \overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{A} \backslash \{ i, j \}} (s) \mathrm{d} s.\)

This implies that \(\{ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}} (t) \}_{\mathcal{A} \subset \mathcal{N}}\) (where \(T \in \mathbb{R}_+\) is such that \(\operatorname{supp} (f_j) \subset [0, T]\)) are equal to \(\{ \overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{A}} (t) \}_{\mathcal{A} \subset \mathcal{N}}\), which is equivalent to saying that \(B_t\) has, with respect to \(\bar{\omega}\), the law of the Brownian motion \(X\) with covariance \(G\). The opposite direction of the implication is trivial.\(\Box\)

Theorem 4.42 is crucial for proving the following non-commutative version of Girsanov's theorem.

Theorem 4.45. (Non-commutative Girsanov's formula). Let \((\mathcal{M}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t)_t)\) be a filtered modular space and let \(X\) be an analytic Brownian motion of covariance \(G\). Consider a process \(H_{\cdot} : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, -}\), where \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) is the Fréchet algebra introduced in Remark 4.40, and let \(Z_t\) be an even adapted process in \(\mathbb{L}^2\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle Z_t = 1 + \int_0^t \langle Z_s \cdot H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle . \) (4.33)

Let \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z\) be the expectation associated with it. Then, the random field, such that

\(\displaystyle B_t (v) = X_t (v) - \int_0^t H_s (G^{\ast} \Theta (v)) \mathrm{d} s, \qquad \forall v \in \mathfrak{h} \quad t \in \mathbb{R}_+,\)

is a Brownian motion of covariance \(G\) with respect to the signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z\).

Remark 4.46. By requiring that \(Z \in \mathbb{L}^2\) satisfies equation (4.33), we automatically get that \(Z\) is a martingale by definition of the Itô integral, see Section 4.1.

Proof Theorem 4.45. The proof follows the same strategy of the commutative case (see, e.g., [Le 16, Section 5.5]). First of all, by (4.32) for the conditional expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z\), we have that the process \(B_t\) is a martingale with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z\) if and only if the process \(B_t Z_t\) is a martingale with respect to the conditional expectation \(\omega_t\). On the other hand, by the Itô formula, the fact that \(Z\) commutes with \(B\) and (4.33), we get

\begin{eqnarray*} B_t (v) Z_t & = & \int_0^t Z_s \mathrm{d} B_s (v) + \int_0^t B_s (v) \mathrm{d} Z_s + [B (v), Z]_t\\ & = & \int_0^t Z_s \mathrm{d} X_s (v) - \int_0^t Z_s H_s (G^{\ast} \Theta v) \mathrm{d} s + \int_0^t \langle B_s (v) Z_s \cdot H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \int_0^t Z_s H_s (G^{\ast} \Theta Gv) \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & \int_0^t Z_s \mathrm{d} X_s + \int_0^t \langle B_s (v) Z_s H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle \end{eqnarray*}

which is a martingale with respect to \(\omega_t\), since it is an Itô integral with respect to \(X\). On the other hand, by Corollary 4.44, the quadratic variation of \(B_t\) with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z\) is

\(\displaystyle [B (v), B (v')]_t = [X (v), X (v')]_t = \langle \Theta v, Gv' \rangle .\)

Thus since \(B\) is a martingale with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z\) with quadratic variation \(\langle \Theta v, Gv' \rangle\), by Theorem 4.42, \(B\) is a weak Brownian motion with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_t^Z\) with covariance \(G\).\(\Box\)

Unlike the commutative case, we do not know if (4.33) has a solution because the exponential of an Itô process in \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) is generally not well-defined. We will now consider some sufficient conditions on \(H\) such that the solution \(Z\) to (4.33) exists.

Definition 4.47. Let \(X\) be an analytic Brownian motion of covariance \(G\). An adapted process \(H_{\cdot} : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^X_-\) satisfies the Novikov condition if for every \(p \geqslant 2\) we have \(\| H_{\cdot} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p, | G |} \in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+)\), and for every \(t > 0\), we get

\(\displaystyle \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \frac{1}{n!} \left\| \left( \int_0^t \langle H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \mathrm{d} s \right)^n \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^p} < \infty .\)

Let \(H_{\cdot} : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}_{X, -}\) be an adapted process satisfying the Novikov condition. We set

\(\displaystyle Z^{(n)}_t := \sum_{j = 0}^n \frac{1}{j!} \left( \int_0^t \langle H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \mathrm{d} s \right)^j .\)

For every \(p \geqslant 1\), \(Z^{(n)}_t\) converges to

\(\displaystyle Z_t = \sum_{j = 0}^{\infty} \frac{1}{j!} \left( \int_0^t \langle H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \mathrm{d} s \right)^j \) (4.34)

in \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{L}^p)\), and \(Z_t\) is an invertible element of \(\mathcal{G}^X_+\) with \(Z^{- 1}_t\) given by \(Z^{- 1}_t = \sum_{j = 0}^{\infty} \frac{(- 1)^j}{j!} \left( \int_0^t \langle H_s, \mathrm{d} B_s \rangle - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \mathrm{d} s \right)^j\).

Lemma 4.48. Let \(X\) be an analytic Brownian motion of covariance \(G\) and let \(H_{\cdot} : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^X_-\) be an adapted process satisfying the Novikov condition. Then, \(Z_t\) defined in (4.34) satisfies (4.33).

Proof. By the Itô formula we get that

\(\displaystyle Z^{(n)}_t = 1 + \int_0^t \langle Z^{(n - 1)}_s \cdot H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t (Z^{(n - 2)}_s - Z^{(n - 1)}_s) \cdot \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \mathrm{d} s. \)

Taking the limit \(n \rightarrow \infty\), which is well-defined by the Novikov condition, we obtain the claim.\(\Box\)

When \(H\) takes values in \(\mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X\) the Novikov condition is automatically satisfied. More precisely, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 4.49. Let \(X\) be an analytic Brownian motion of covariance \(G\) and let \(H_{\cdot} : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^X_-\) be an adapted process such that \(\operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_{\cdot} \otimes H_{\cdot}) \in L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\). Then, \(H\) satisfies the Novikov condition.

Proof. We note that for any \(n \in \mathbb{N}\)

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{n!} \left( \int_0^t \langle H_s, \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \mathrm{d} s \right)^n = Z^{(n)}_t - Z^{(n - 1)}_t . \) (4.35)

By Theorem 4.5, equation (4.48) and Hölder's inequality for the twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces we get

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| Z^{(n)}_t - Z^{(n + 1)}_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}\\ \, \leqslant C_p \int_0^t \| Z^{(n - 1)}_s - Z^{(n)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} (\| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}})^{\frac{1}{2}} \mathrm{d} s\\ \qquad + \left. \frac{C_p}{2} \int_0^t (\| (Z^{(n - 2)}_s - Z^{(n - 1)}) \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} + \| Z^{(n - 1)}_s - Z^{(n)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}) \| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} \mathrm{d} s \right) \end{array} \) (4.36)

for a suitable constant \(C_p > 0\) (depending on \(p \geqslant 2\)). By induction, it is simple to see that

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in [0, T]} \| Z^{(n)}_t - Z^{(n + 1)}_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \leqslant \frac{(3 C_p (1 + \| \operatorname{Tr}_{G^{\ast} \Theta} (H_s \otimes H_s) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}) (T + 1))^n}{n!}, \)

hence the claim.\(\Box\)

4.4Weak solutions of finite-dimensional Grassmann SDE

In this section we define the notion of weak and strong solution to an Grassmann stochastic differential equation (SDE), when the vector space \(\mathfrak{h}\) is finite dimensional. First of all we introduce some notation. We note that the space \(\mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X = \overline{\mathcal{G}}_X \cap \mathbb{L}^{\infty}\) is a Banach algebra, thus if an adapted process \(B\) belongs to \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\) we can consider the Banach algebra \(\mathcal{G}^{\infty}_B\) as the natural Banach subalgebra generated by \(B\) in \(\mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X\).

We now consider two linear functions

\(\displaystyle \mu : \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \bigoplus_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \Lambda^{2 n + 1} \mathfrak{h}, \quad \sigma : \mathfrak{h} \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \left( \bigoplus_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \Lambda^{2 n} \mathfrak{h} \right) . \) (4.37)

Definition 4.50. In a standard setting, let \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) a signed expectation on \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_X\) and consider two linear functions \(\mu, \sigma\) as in (4.37), we say that the couple of adapted Itô odd random fields \((\Psi_{\cdot}, B_{\cdot})\) is a weak solution to the SDE \((\mu, \sigma)\) (with respect the signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\)) on \([0, T]\) and with initial condition \(\Psi_0\) if \(B\) is a weak Brownian motion with covariance \(G\) with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\) and, for any \(v \in \mathfrak{h}\) and \(t \leqslant T\), the following equation holds:

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t (v) - \Psi_0 (v) = \int_0^t \mu (v) (\Psi_s) \mathrm{d} s + \int_0^t \langle \sigma (v, \cdot) (\Psi_s), \mathrm{d} B_s \rangle .\)

Furthermore, if \(B \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\) we say that \(\Psi\) is a strong solution to the SDE \((\mu, \sigma)\) driven by \(B\) (till the time \(T\)) if \((\Psi, B)\) is a weak solution to the SDE \((\mu, \sigma)\) and \(\Psi_t \in \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_B\) for any \(0 \leqslant t \leqslant T\).

In the special case where \(\sigma (v, v') = \langle v, v' \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}\) (i.e. additive noise SDEs) and \(\mathfrak{h}\) is finite-dimensional, we can prove the existence of strong solutions.

Theorem 4.51. Suppose that \(\mathfrak{h}\) is finite-dimensional and let \(\sigma (v, v') = \langle v, v' \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}\) then for any \(\Psi_0 \in \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_{X, -} = \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X \cap \overline{\mathcal{G}}_{X, -}\), \(B \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\) Brownian motion with respect to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}\), and \(T \geqslant 0\), there is a unique strong solution \(\Psi_t \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_B)\) to the SDE \((\mu, \sigma)\), driven by \(B\).

Proof. This theorem, using a slightly difference language and notations, has been proved in [ABDG22, Theorem 30 and 31], in the case where \(\| B_t - B_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} \lesssim | t - s |^{\frac{1}{2}}\). The case where the map \(t \mapsto B_t\) is continuous in \(\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\) can be proved in a similar way. \(\Box\)

Here we introduce the notation

\(\displaystyle \mu^A (v) := \mu (v) - A v,\)

where \(A : \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathfrak{h}\) is a linear map.

Theorem 4.52. Suppose that \(\mathfrak{h}\) is finite-dimensional, suppose that \(\bar{G} = G^{\ast} \Theta\) is an invertible antilinear map, and consider \(\sigma (v, v') = \langle v, v' \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}\). Write

\(\displaystyle X^A_t (v) = \tilde{X}_0 (\mathrm{e}^{At} v) + \int_0^t \langle \mathrm{e}^{A (t - s)} (v), \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle .\)

and consider the processes

\begin{eqnarray*} Z_t^{\mu^A} & = & \exp \left( \int_0^t \boldsymbol{1}_{[0, T]} (s) \langle \mu^A (\bar{G}^{- 1} \cdot, (X_s^A + \mathrm{e}^{A s} h_0)), \mathrm{d} X_s \rangle + \right.\\ & & \left. - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \boldsymbol{1}_{[0, T]} (s) \operatorname{Tr}_{\bar{G}} (\mu^A (\bar{G}^{- 1} \cdot, (X_s^A + \mathrm{e}^{A s} h_0)) \otimes \mu^A (\bar{G}^{- 1} \cdot, (X_s^A + \mathrm{e}^{A s} h_0))) \mathrm{d} s \right)\\ B_t (\cdot) & = & \int_0^t \boldsymbol{1}_{[0, T]} (s) \mu^A (\cdot, (X_s^A + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A s} \cdot))) \mathrm{d} s + X_t . \end{eqnarray*}

The the pair of processes \((X^A + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A \cdot}), B)\) is a weak solution of the SDE \((\mu, \sigma)\) on \([0, T]\) with initial condition \(\tilde{X}_0 + h_0\) and with respect to the expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{Z^{\mu^A}}\). Furthermore \(X^A_t + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A t})\) coincides with the strong solution of Theorem 4.51.

Proof. First we note that the process \(Z_t^{\mu^A}\) is well-defined. Indeed, \(\mu^A (\bar{G}^{- 1} \cdot, (X_s^A + \mathrm{e}^{A s} h_0)) \in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}_X^{\infty})\) and by Proposition 4.49 it satisfies the Novikov condition. Furthermore, the process \(X^A_t + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A t})\) solves the SDE \(X_t^A (v) + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A t} v) - \tilde{X}_0 (v) - h_0 (v) = \int_0^t (X^A_s (A v) + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A t} A v)) \mathrm{d} s + X_t (v)\). Which means that we have the following relation, for any \(t \leqslant T\)

\(\displaystyle X_t^A (v) + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A t} v) - \tilde{X}_0 (v) - h_0 (v) = \int_0^t \mu (v, X^A_s + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A s} \cdot)) \mathrm{d} s + B_t (v) . \)

Since, by Theorem 4.45, \(B_t\) is a Brownian motion with respect to the expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{Z_t^{\mu^A}}\), equation (4.52) implies that \((X^A + h_0 (\mathrm{e}^{A \cdot}), B)\) is a weak solution to the SDE \((\mu, \sigma)\). Finally, since by Theorem 4.51 the strong solution exists and is unique, we have \(\mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X = \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_B\) so that any weak solution is also a strong solution.\(\Box\)

5Applications

5.1Twisted \(L^p\) bound of the exponential

In Subsection 5.1 we prove the existence of a family of weak Gibbsian quartic perturbation of \(\omega\), a special case being the Grassmann \(\Psi^4_2\) on the torus. The brevity of the arguments presented should convince the reader about the usefulness of the twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces introduced in Section 2.3. Our strategy is based on the control of the exponential of a Wick's polynomial in the spirit of the well-known bound by Nelson [Nel66, Sim74].

In order to consider spin-\(\frac{1}{2}\) fermionic fields on \(\mathbb{T}^2\), we let \(\mathfrak{h} = L^2 (\mathbb{T}^2 ; \mathbb{C}^4)\) and let \((\mathcal{M}^{(\mu)}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}^{(\mu)}_t)_{t \geqslant 0})\) be the filtered modular space associated with \(h\), see Section 3.2. For the sake of brevity, we henceforth fix \(\mu = \frac{1}{2}\) and remove it from our notation. We let \((\Psi_t)_t\) be a GBM with covariance

\(\displaystyle G_t = (1 - \Delta)^{\theta - 1} \chi (| - \Delta | / t) (\mathbb{1 \oplus - 1}), \) (5.1)

where \(- \Delta\) is the Laplacian on \(h\), where \(\theta \geqslant 0\) and where \(\chi \in C^{\infty}_c (\mathbb{R}^+)\) is such that \(\chi ([0, 1 / 2]) = 1\) and \(\operatorname{supp} (\chi) = [0, 1]\). We let \(\delta_{x, t} (y) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} k (y - x)} \chi (| k | / t)\) together with \(\delta_{x, t, +} = (\delta_{x, t}, 0)\) and \(\delta_{x, t, -} = (0, \delta_{x, t})\), and introduce

\(\displaystyle \bar{\Psi}_{t, \sigma} (x) := \Psi_t (\delta_{x, t, \sigma} \oplus 0), \qquad \Psi_{t, \sigma} (x) := \Psi_t (0 \oplus \delta_{x, t, \sigma}) \qquad \sigma \in \{ \pm \} .\)

Note that

\(\displaystyle \omega (\Psi_t (x) \bar{\Psi}_t (y)) = G_t (x ; y) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} k (x - y)} \chi (| k | / t)}{(1 + k^2)^{1 - \theta}} 1, \) (5.2)

and that by Proposition 3.17, if \(\theta > 0\)

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x, \sigma} \| \Psi_{t, \sigma} (x) \|^2 \lesssim \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \frac{\chi (| k | / t)}{(1 + k^2)^{1 - \theta}} \sim t^{2 \theta} \) (5.3)

whereas the divergence is as \(\sim \log t\) if \(\theta = 0\). We prove the existence of weak Gibbsian quartic perturbation of \(\omega\), for \(\theta\) small enough.

Theorem 5.1. For any \(\lambda \in \mathbb{C}\) and \(t \geqslant 0\) define \(Z_t^{(\lambda)} = \exp (\lambda V_t)\) with

\(\displaystyle V_t := \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (\bar{\Psi}_t (x), \Psi_t (x))^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x,\)

having set \((\bar{\Psi}_t (x), \Psi_t (x)) = \sum_{\sigma = \pm} \bar{\Psi}_{t, \sigma} (x) \Psi_{t, \sigma} (x)\). Then, if \(7 \theta < 1\) and if \(| \lambda |\) is small enough depending on \(\mu\), the weak limit

\(\displaystyle \omega^{(\lambda)} (\cdot) := \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \frac{\omega \left( \cdot \, Z_t^{ (\lambda)} \right)}{\omega (Z_t^{(\lambda)})}, \) (5.4)

is a well-defined continuous normalized linear functional on \(\mathbb{L}^p\) for any \(1 < p \leqslant \infty\).

Remark 5.2. In particular, \(\omega^{(\lambda)}\) is a signed expectation on the Fréchet algebra \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{\Psi}\), see Definition 4.37 and Remark 4.40. The fermionic \(\Psi^4_2\) theory corresponds to the choice \(\theta = 0\). Note that the analysis we present can be easily generalized to the case of a general polynomial interaction and of any dimension, provided that the covariance is suitably adjusted.

The claim in Theorem 5.1 is a straightforward consequence of Theorem 5.3 and Corollary 5.5 below. The crucial point here is to show that even though \(\lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} Z_t^{(\lambda)}\) is not a bounded random variable, it belongs to the twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces for any \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\).

Theorem 5.3. Let \(7 \theta < 1\). Then, for any \(\lambda \in \mathbb{C}\) we have \(Z_t^{(\lambda)} \in \bigcap_{p < \infty} \mathbb{L}^p\) uniformly in \(t \geqslant 0\) and \(Z_t^{(\lambda)} \rightarrow Z^{(\lambda)}\) as \(t \rightarrow \infty\) in the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) topology, for any \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\). Furthermore, for any \(x \in \mathbb{L}^p\), \(1 < p \leqslant \infty\), \(\omega (x \cdot Z_t^{(\lambda)})\) has a limit as \(t \rightarrow \infty\).

The following technical lemma will be used in the proof of Theorem 5.3.

Lemma 5.4. The following bounds hold true for any \(\nu < 1 - 3 \theta\)

\(\displaystyle \| V_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} \lesssim t^{4 \theta}, \hspace{6em} \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V_t - V_s) \|_2^2 \lesssim_{\tau} s^{- 2 \nu},\)

where the constant in \(\lesssim_{\tau}\) is locally bounded in \(\tau\).

Proof. To prove the first bound, we write the Wick's polynomial explicitly

\(\displaystyle \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (\bar{\Psi}_t (x), \Psi_t (x))^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} = (\bar{\Psi}_t (x), \Psi_t (x))^2 + 2 C_t (\bar{\Psi}_t (x), \Psi_t (x)) + 2 C_t^2\)

with \(C_t := \omega (\Psi_{t, \sigma} (x) \bar{\Psi}_{t, \sigma} (x)) \sim t^{2 \theta}\), see (5.2), and therefore, by (5.3) and by Lemma 3.19 we obtain the bound

\(\displaystyle \| V_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} \lesssim \sup_{x \in \mathbb{T}^2} \| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (\bar{\Psi}_t (x), \Psi_t (x))^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \| \lesssim t^{4 \theta} .\)

To prove the other bound, we follow the strategy of [Seg86], see also [Sim74], and switch to the Fourier components:

\(\displaystyle V_t = \sum_{k_1, \ldots, k_4 \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \boldsymbol{1}_{\sum_i k_i = 0} \left( \prod_i \chi (| k_i | / t) \right) \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (\widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_t (k_1), \hat{\Psi}_t (k_2)) (\widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_t (k_3), \hat{\Psi}_t (k_4)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}},\)

where now, denoting spinorial plane waves by \(e_{k, \sigma}^+ := e_{k, \sigma} \oplus 0\), \(e_{k, \sigma}^- := 0 \oplus e_{k, \sigma}\), with \(e_{k, +} (y) = (\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} ky}, 0)\) and \(e_{k, -} (y) = (0, \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} ky})\), we set \(\hat{\Psi}_{t, \sigma} (k ) := \Psi_t (e^-_{k, \sigma})\) and \(\widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_{t, \sigma} (k) = \Psi_t (e^+_{k, \sigma})\). With the notation introduced in Lemma 3.15, we also set \(e^{\ell}_{k, \sigma, t} := \mathcal{C}_t e^{\ell}_{k, \sigma}\) together with \(\tilde{e}^{\ell}_{k, \sigma, t} := \tilde{\mathcal{C}}_t e^{\ell}_{k, \sigma}\) and note that, for some constant \(C\)

\(\displaystyle \langle e^{\ell}_{k, \sigma, t}, e^{\ell'}_{k', \sigma', s} \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = \langle \tilde{e}^{\ell}_{k, \sigma, t}, \tilde{e}^{\ell'}_{k', \sigma', s} \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = C \delta_{\ell, \ell'} \delta_{\sigma, \sigma'} \delta_{k, k'} \frac{\chi (| k | / (t \wedge s))}{(1 + k^2)^{1 - \theta}} . \) (5.5)

Then, by Lemma 3.19, noting that the r.h.s. of (5.5) depends only on \(r = t \wedge s\), we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \langle T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_{t, \sigma} (k_1) \hat{\Psi}_{t, \sigma} (k_2) \widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_{t, \rho} (k_3) \hat{\Psi}_{t, \rho} (k_4) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}), \\ \qquad \qquad \qquad T^{(2)}_{\tau} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_{s, \sigma'} (k_1') \hat{\Psi}_{s, \sigma'} (k_2') \widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_{s, \rho'} (k_3') \hat{\Psi}_{s, \rho'} (k_4') \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \rangle_{L^2}\\ = C_{\tau} \langle (e_{k_1, \sigma, r}^+ \oplus \tilde{e}_{k_1, \sigma, r}^+) \wedge \cdots \wedge (e_{k_4, \rho, r}^- \oplus \tilde{e}_{k_4, \rho, r}^-), \\ \qquad \qquad \qquad (e_{k_1', \sigma', r}^+ \oplus \tilde{e}_{k_1', \sigma', r}^+) \wedge \cdots \wedge (e_{k_4', \rho', r}^- \oplus \tilde{e}_{k_4', \rho', r}^-) \rangle_{\Gamma_a (\mathcal{H} \oplus \mathcal{H})} \end{array} \) (5.6)

for some constant \(C_{\tau}\) locally bounded in \(\tau\). Therefore, combining (5.5) and (5.6), we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V_t - V_s) \|_2^2 = \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V_t) \|_2^2 - \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V_s) \|_2^2\\ \quad = C_{\tau} \sum_{k_1, \ldots, k_4 \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \left( \prod_i \chi (| k_i | / t) - \prod_i \chi (| k_i | / s) \right) \frac{\boldsymbol{1}_{\sum_i k_i = 0}}{\prod_i (1 + k_i^2)^{1 - \theta}}, \end{array}\)

for some other constant \(C_{\tau}\) locally bounded in \(\tau\). By the Young's inequality for convolutions we obtain, for any \(\varepsilon > 0\)

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V_t - V_s) \|_2^2 \lesssim_{\tau} \| f_{s, t} \|_{L^p (\mathbb{R}^2)} \| f_{0, t} \|_{L^q (\mathbb{R}^2)}^3 \lesssim s^{- 2 \nu} \) (5.7)

where \(\lesssim_{\tau}\) is up to a constant locally bounded in \(\tau\), where

\(\displaystyle q = \frac{1 + \varepsilon}{1 - \theta}, \hspace{3em} p^{- 1} = 3 \left( \frac{\varepsilon + \theta}{1 + \varepsilon} \right), \hspace{3em} \nu = \frac{1 - 2 \varepsilon - 3 \theta}{1 + \varepsilon},\)

and where \(f_{s, t} (k) = (\chi (| k_i | / t) - \chi (| k_i | / s)) (1 + k^2)^{\theta - 1}\) is such that \(\| f_{s, t} \|_{L^q (\mathbb{R}^2)} \lesssim 1\) uniformly in \(s, t \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(\varepsilon > 0\). This concludes the proof.\(\Box\)

Proof of Theorem 5.3. By Hölder's inequality for the twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces, we have

\(\displaystyle \| Z_t^{(\lambda)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \leqslant \sum_{n \geqslant 0} \frac{| \lambda |^n \| V_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^{pn}}^n}{n!} . \) (5.8)

By Lemma 5.4 and by the hypercontractivity bounds, see Lemma 3.23, noting that \(C_{\frac{p}{p - 1}, \varrho = \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{1_{\mathcal{H}}}} \lesssim p^{\frac{1}{2}}\), we have for \(p \geqslant 2\)

\(\displaystyle \| T^{(p)}_{\tau} (V_t - V_s) \|_p \lesssim_{\tau} p^2 s^{- \nu} . \) (5.9)

Therefore, by Proposition 2.19, by Lemma 5.4 and by (5.9) we obtain for any \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\)

\(\displaystyle \| V_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \leqslant \| V_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} + \| V_t - V_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{p \vee 2}} \lesssim s^{4 \theta} + p^2 s^{- \nu} .\)

In particular, choosing \(s = p^{\frac{2}{4 \theta + \nu}}\) we have \(\| V_t \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \lesssim p^{\frac{8 \theta}{4 \theta + \nu}}\), which by (5.8) and by the fact that \(7 \theta < 1\) implies

\(\displaystyle \| Z_t^{(\lambda)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \leqslant \sum_{n \geqslant 0} \frac{| \lambda |^n c^n (pn)^{\frac{8 \theta n}{4 \theta + \nu}}}{n!} < \infty,\)

for some constant \(c\) and for any \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\), hence \(Z_t^{(\lambda)} \in \bigcap_{p < \infty} \mathbb{L}^p\) uniformly in \(t \geqslant 0\). To prove that the limit exists, we use that \(V_t\) are commuting random variables and by the fundamental theorem of calculus write

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{e}^{\lambda V_t} - \mathrm{e}^{\lambda V_s} = \lambda \int_0^1 \mathrm{e}^{\lambda V_t (1 - r)} (V_t - V_s) \mathrm{e}^{\lambda V_s r} \mathrm{d} r.\)

Therefore, for any \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\) and any \(s \leqslant t\)

\(\displaystyle \| Z_t^{(\lambda)} - Z_s^{(\lambda)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \leqslant | \lambda | (\sup_{r = s, t} \| Z_r^{(\lambda)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 p}}) \| V_t - V_s \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 p}} \lesssim_{\lambda, p} s^{- \nu},\)

proving the continuity in the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) topology. The continuity of \(\omega (x \cdot Z_t^{(\lambda)})\) for \(x \in \mathbb{L}^p\), \(1 < p \leqslant \infty\) follows by the fact that \(x \cdot Z_t^{(\lambda)} \in \mathbb{L}^1\) for any \(t\) and that

\(\displaystyle | \omega (x \cdot Z_t^{(\lambda)}) - \omega (x \cdot Z_s^{(\lambda)}) | = | \omega (x \cdot (Z_t^{(\lambda)} - Z_s^{(\lambda)})) | \leqslant \| x \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \| Z_t^{(\lambda)} - Z_t^{(\lambda)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^{p'}},\)

where \({p'}^{- 1} = 1 - p^{- 1} < \infty\).\(\Box\)

On the other hand, the normalization factor can be controlled for small \(| \lambda |\).

Corollary 5.5. If \(| \lambda |\) is small enough, then \(| 1 - \omega (Z_t^{(\lambda)}) | \lesssim | \lambda |\) for any \(t \geqslant 0\). Furthermore, the limit \(\lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \omega (Z_t^{(\lambda)})\) exists.

Proof. Along the lines of the proof of Theorem 5.3, by using the properties of the twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces we have

\(\displaystyle | 1 - \omega (Z_t^{(\lambda)}) | = | \omega (1 - Z_t^{(\lambda)}) | \leqslant \| 1 - Z_t^{(\lambda)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^1} \leqslant \sum_{n \geqslant 1} \frac{| \lambda |^n c^n n^{\frac{8 \theta n}{4 \theta + \nu}}}{n!} \lesssim_{\theta} | \lambda | .\)

Furthermore, for any \(s \leqslant t\)

\(\displaystyle | \omega (Z_t^{(\lambda)}) - \omega (Z_s^{(\lambda)}) | = | \omega (Z_t^{(\lambda)} - Z_s^{(\lambda)}) | \leqslant \| Z_t^{(\lambda)} - Z_s^{(\lambda)} \|_{\mathbb{L}^1} \lesssim s^{- \nu},\)

hence the existence of the limit.\(\Box\)

5.2Weak solution to \(\Psi^4_2\) stochastic quantization SPDE

In this section we provide the solution to the stochastic quantization equation for a fermionic \(\Psi^4_2\) on the two-dimensional torus \(\mathbb{T}^2\) (namely the fermionic SPDE having the signed expectation built in Section 5.1 as invariant solution), in the same way in which Jona-Lasinio and Mitter [JM85] proved the existence of a weak solution in the bosonic case. More precisely, our strategy is as follows. First, we give a finite dimensional approximation of the \(\Psi^4_2\) stochastic quantization equation (5.10). Thanks to this finite-dimensional approximation, see (5.13), we can apply the tools developed in Sections 4.2-4.3, namely the Itô and Girsanov's formulas, to provide a weak solution to (5.13), compare with Section 4.4. Finally, we prove that the process \(Z^N\), i.e. the density of the signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{Z^N}\) that appears in Girsanov's formula, converges to a well-defined random variable \(Z\) as the \(N \rightarrow \infty\). As a result, we obtain a weak solution to the original equation (5.10) under the signed expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^Z\).

Let us now delve into the details of the construction. We consider \(\mathfrak{h} = L^2 (\mathbb{T}^2 ; \mathbb{C}^4)\) and an analytic Brownian motion \(X_t\) with covariance \(U = (\mathbb{1 \oplus - 1})\). Hereafter if \(\chi : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\) we write \(\chi_t (x) = (\chi^1_t (x), \bar{\chi}^1_t (x), \chi^2_t (x), \bar{\chi}^2_t (x))\) to be the limit, if exists,

\(\displaystyle \chi^j_t (x) := \lim_{\tau \rightarrow + \infty} \chi_t (\delta_{x, \tau, j} \oplus 0), \quad \bar{\chi}^j_t (x) := \lim_{\tau \rightarrow + \infty} \chi_t (0 \oplus \delta_{x, \tau, j}),\)

which has to be understood in \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\) (see Appendix A for the definitions of \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\) and \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\)). In this way, if \(v (x) = (v^1 (x), \bar{v}^1 (x), v^2 (x), \bar{v}^2 (x)) \in C^{\infty} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathbb{C}^4) \subset \mathfrak{h}\), we have

\(\displaystyle \chi_t (v) = \sum_{j = 1, 2} (\langle \chi^j_t (\cdot), v^j (\cdot) \rangle_{\mathcal{S}', \mathcal{S}} + \langle \bar{\chi}^j_t (\cdot), \bar{v}^j (\cdot) \rangle_{\mathcal{S}', \mathcal{S}}) .\)

Using the described identification, when possible, of \(\chi : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathfrak{h} \rightarrow \mathcal{G}^p_X\) with the function \(\chi : \mathbb{R}_+ \rightarrow \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X)^4\) allows us to speak about the Besov regularity of a stochastic process. In particular, we say that a stochastic process \(\chi : \mathbb{R}_+ \rightarrow \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\ell}_X)^k\) has Besov regularity \(B^s_{p, q}\) if \(\chi_{\cdot} (\cdot) \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\ell}_X))\) - see Appendix A for the definitions of space of distributions and Besov spaces taking values in a Banach space.

Remark 5.6. In the particular case where \(\chi_{\cdot} (\cdot) \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, B^s_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X))\), we can consider the following sequence of stronger seminorms, for any \(T \geqslant 0\)

\(\displaystyle \| \chi \|_{C^0 ([0, T], B^s_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X))}^p \lesssim \sup_{0 \leqslant t \leqslant T} \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{p s j} \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \| K_j \ast \chi (x) \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}^p \mathrm{d} x,\)

where \(K_j = \mathcal{F}^{- 1}_{\mathbb{T}^2} (\varphi_j)\), \(j \geqslant - 1\), and \(\{ \varphi_j \}_{j \geqslant - 1}\) is a dyadic partition of unity of \(\mathbb{R}^2\), see Appendix A.

Using the notation introduced above, the \(\Psi^4_2\) stochastic quantization SPDE reads, for any \(0 \leqslant \theta < \frac{1}{2}\), \(\lambda \in \mathbb{R}_+\),

\(\displaystyle \chi_t (x) = \chi_0 (x) - \int_0^t (A^{1 - 2 \theta} \chi_s (x) + \lambda A^{- 2 \theta} (\chi_s | \chi_s |^2) (x) - \lambda \infty A^{- 2 \theta} \chi_s) \mathrm{d} s + A^{- \theta} X_t (x), \quad x \in \mathbb{T}^2, \) (5.10)

where \(A = (- \Delta + m^2)\), where

\(\displaystyle | \chi |^2 = \sum_{j = 1, 2} \chi^1 \bar{\chi}^1, \) (5.11)

\(\chi = (\chi^1, \bar{\chi}^1, \chi^2, \bar{\chi}^2) \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\ell}_X)^4)\) (for some \(\ell \geqslant 3\)) and \(X_t\) is some Brownian motion of covariance \(U\), and the subtraction of \(- \infty A^{- 2 \theta} \chi_s\) stands for a renormalisation procedure which will be explained below. As usual, the necessity of a renormalisation procedure is due to the low expected regularity of the solution to the SPDE (5.10).

Lemma 5.7. For any \(0 \leqslant \theta < \frac{1}{2}\), consider the process

\(\displaystyle X_t^A = \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} \tilde{X}_0 + \int_0^t \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} (t - s)} A^{- \theta} \mathrm{d} X_s, \) (5.12)

where \(X_0\) is an analytic (odd) Gaussian with covariance \(A^{- 1} U\) independent of the process \((X_t)_{t \geqslant 0}\), then for any, \(\varepsilon > 0,\) and \(2 \leqslant p \leqslant \infty\) we have \(X^A \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, B^{- \varepsilon}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X))\).

Proof. The proof is given in the case \(p = \infty\) in [ABDG22, Lemma 62]. The generic \(p \geqslant 2\) can be proved using a similar method and hypercontractivity of Gaussian random variables of Theorem 3.20 (see also [DD03] for an analogous proof in the commutative case).\(\Box\)

Notation 5.8. Let \(\mathfrak{Q (\chi, \psi)}\) be a (local) antisymmetric polynomial of the (regular) random fields, we denote by \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (\chi, \psi) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) the same polynomial where every product between the components of \(\chi\) and \(\psi\) is replaced by the Wick product, where \(\chi, \psi\) are Gaussian random field, more precisely we suppose \(\chi = X^A_t\) and \(\psi = A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t\).

For example if \(\mathfrak{Q} (\chi, \psi) = P_N (\chi^1) (x) P_N (\bar{\chi}^1) (x) P_N (\chi^2) (x) P_N (\bar{\chi}^2) (x)\), where \(P_N\) is the (\(L^2 (\mathbb{T}^2)\)-)orthogonal projection on the Fourier modes less or equal then \(N \geqslant 0\), and recalling that \(\mathcal{E} (P_N (X^{A, 1}_t) (x) P_N (\bar{X}^{A, 1}_t) (x)) = \mathcal{E} (P_N (X^{A, 1}_t) (x) P_N (\bar{X}^{A, 1}_t) (x)) = C_N\) where \(C_N = \sum_{| k | \leqslant N} \frac{1}{| k |^2 + m^2}\) and \(\mathcal{E} (X^{A, i} (x) \bar{X}^{A, j} (x)) = 0\), for any \(i, j = 1, 2\) and \(i \not{=} j\), we have

\(\displaystyle \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (\chi, \psi) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} = \chi^1 (x) \bar{\chi}^1 (x) \chi^2 (x) \bar{\chi}^2 (x) - C_N \chi^1 (x) \bar{\chi}^1 (x) - C_N \chi^2 (x) \bar{\chi}^2 (x) .\)

Using the symbols introduced in Notation 5.8, we consider an approximate stochastic quantization equation for \(x \in \mathbb{T}^2\)

\(\displaystyle \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} \chi_t^{(N)} (x) & = & \chi_0^{(N)} (x) - \int_0^t \left[ A^{1 - 2 \theta} \chi_s^{(N)} (x) + \lambda P_N \left( A^{- 2 \theta} \left\llbracket \chi^{(N)}_s | \chi_s^{(N)} |^2 \right\rrbracket \right) (x) \right] \mathrm{d} s + A^{- \theta} X_t (x), \hspace{3cm} \text{(5.13)} \\ \chi^{(N)}_0 (x) & = & \tilde{X}_0 (x) + h_0 (x) . \end{array} \right.\)

Remark 5.9. We take an initial condition of the form Gaussian free field plus some regular random variable, since we want to cover the case where the initial condition is distributed as the non-commutative measure \(\omega^{(\lambda)}\) defined in Theorem 5.1. The fact that a non-commutative random variable distributed as \(\omega^{(\lambda)}\) can be realized as a regular shift of a Gaussian free field is proved in [DFG22].

Let us now consider the process

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} Z^{N, h_0}_t = \exp \left( \lambda \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} P_N (A^{- \theta} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{P}_3 (P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) (x) \mathrm{d} X_s (x) \right.\\ \qquad \left. - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \langle P_N (A^{- \theta} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{P}_3 (P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}), \right. \left. U A^{- \theta} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{P}_3 (P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \rangle_{\mathbb{R}^4} \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} s \right), \end{array}\)

where

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{P}_3 (P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0)) = P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) | P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) |^2,\)

and the Itô random field

\(\displaystyle B^{N, h_0}_t (x) = X_t (x) + \int_0^t P_N (A^{- 2 \theta} (P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) | P_N (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) |^2)) (x) \mathrm{d} s.\)

The reason of the introduction of the previous processes is the following weak representation of the solution to the approximating SPDE (5.13).

Proposition 5.10. For any \(h_0 \in C^1 (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\) and for any \(\varepsilon > 0\), there is a unique (global in time) strong solution to \(\chi^{(N)}_t \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, C^{- \varepsilon} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X))\) to equation (5.13) driven by the Brownian motion \(X_t\). Furthermore the couple of processes \((X^A + \mathrm{e}^{A^{1 - 2 \theta} \cdot} h_0, B^{N, h_0})\) is a weak solution to the SPDE (5.13) with respect to the expectation \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{Z^{N, h_0}} (\cdot) = \mathcal{E}_0 (\cdot Z_T^{N, h_0})\), namely we have that, for any polynomial \(F \in \bigoplus_{n = 0}^k \Lambda^n (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^2)^r)\) and for any \(t_1 < \ldots < t_r \in \mathbb{R}_+\),

\(\displaystyle \omega_0 (F (\chi_{t_1}^{(N)}, \ldots, \chi_{t_r}^{(N)}))\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \overline{\mathcal{E}}_0^{Z^{N, h_0}} (F (X^A_{t_1} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_1} h_0, \ldots, X^A_{t_r} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_r} h_0))\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \mathcal{E}_0 (F (X^A_{t_1} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_1} h_0, \ldots, X^A_{t_r} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_r} h_0) Z_{t_r}^{N, h_0}) .\) (5.14)

Proof. The result follows from Theorems 4.51 and 4.52 by noting that it is possible to split equation (5.13) into two independent equations, by projecting the solution on the image of the projection \(P_N\) and \(I - P_N\). The first equation is a non-linear finite dimensional equation, and thus Theorems 4.51 and 4.52 apply directly. The second equation is the linear equation (5.12) projected on the image of \(I - P_N\). A linear equation of the form (5.12) has always global in time solution and, by the independence from the processes \(P_N (\chi^{(N)}_t)\) and \(Z^{N, h_0}\), equality (5.14) can be checked directly. \(\Box\)

In order to take the limit \(N \rightarrow \infty\), in the weak solution \((X^A, B^{N, h_0})\) and obtaining a weak solution to the limit equation (5.10), we first need a result on the regularity of Wick polynomials of \(X^A_t\).

Lemma 5.11. Let \(\mathfrak{Q} (\chi, \psi)\) be any antisymmetric local polynomial of the random fields \(\chi, \psi\) of degree \(n\), which is at most of first degree in \(\psi\). Then, for any \(\frac{2 n (n - 2) + 3}{4 n (n - 2) + 8} < \theta < \frac{1}{2}\), \(p \geqslant 2\) and \(0 \leqslant s < \frac{2 n (n - 2) + 4}{n} \theta - \frac{2 n (n - 2) + 3}{2 n}\), the sequence of random fields \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) is a Cauchy sequence in \(C^0 \left( \mathbb{R}_+, B^{s - \frac{1}{2}}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X) \right)\). We denote the limit by \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (X^A_t, A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\). If \(\mathfrak{Q}\) does not depend on \(\psi\), then \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \rightarrow \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (X_t^A) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) in \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, B^{- \varepsilon}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X))\) for any \(\varepsilon > 0.\) Finally, we have that, for any \(T \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in [0, T]} \| \mathfrak{Q} (X^A_t, A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t) - \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \|_{B^{s - \frac{1}{2}}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X)} {\lesssim p^{\nu (\theta, s)}} \) (5.15)
\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in [0, T]} \| \mathfrak{Q} (X^A_t) - \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t)) \|_{B^{- \varepsilon}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X)} {\lesssim p^{\tilde{\nu} (\theta, \varepsilon)}} \) (5.16)

for some \(\nu (\theta, s), \tilde{\nu} (\theta, \varepsilon) < 1\), not depending on \(T\).

Proof. We give the proof for the case where \(\mathfrak{Q} (\chi, \psi)\) is a monomial of the form \(\mathfrak{Q} (\chi, \psi) = \psi^r \prod_{k_1 = 1}^{n_2} \chi^{j_{k_1}} \prod_{k_2 = 1}^{n_2} \bar{\chi}^{j_{k_2}}\) or \(\mathfrak{Q} (\chi, \psi) = \prod_{k_1 = 1}^{n_2} \chi^{j_{k_1}} \prod_{k_2 = 1}^{n_2} \bar{\chi}^{j_{k_2}}\), for some \(n_1, n_2, j_{k_1}, j_{k_2}, r \in \mathbb{N}\). By linearity the general result follows.

Let us call

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rll} V^{j, r}_N & = & \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \left\llbracket P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^{A, r}_t) \prod_{k_1 = 1}^{n_2} P_N \left( \bar{X}^{A, j_{k_1}}_t \right) \prod_{k_2 = 1}^{n_2} P_N \left( \bar{X}^{A, j_{k_2}}_t \right) \right\rrbracket (x) K_j (x) \mathrm{d} x,\\ V^j_N & = & \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \left\llbracket \prod_{k_1 = 1}^{n_2} P_N \left( \bar{X}^{A, j_{k_1}}_t \right) \prod_{k_2 = 1}^{n_2} P_N \left( \bar{X}^{A, j_{k_2}}_t \right) \right\rrbracket (x) K_j (x) \mathrm{d} x, \end{array}\)

where \(K_j = \mathcal{F}^{- 1} (\varphi_j)\) is the function corresponding to the \(j\)-th Littlewood-Paley block.

By the invariance of the law, and of the norm of \(P_N (X^A_t)\) and \(P_N (A^{1 - \theta} \bar{X}^A_t)\) with respect to spatial and temporal translation, we have that, for any \(N, N' \in \mathbb{N}\),

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in [0, T]} \| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} - \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_{N'} (X^A_t), P_{N'} (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \|_{B^{s - \frac{1}{2}}_{p, p}}^p\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{r = 1}^4 \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{\left( s - \frac{1}{2} \right) j p} \| V^{j, r}_N - V_{N'}^{j, r} \|^p_{\mathbb{L}^p} \sup_{t \in [0, T]} \| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \|_{B^{s - \frac{1}{2}}_{p, p}}^p\) (5.17)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{r = 1}^4 \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{- \left( s - \frac{1}{2} \right) j p} \| V^{j, r}_N \|^p_{\mathbb{L}^p} \mathrm{d} x,\) (5.18)

and similar expression with \(V^j_N\). Since \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), is a polynomial of \(P_N (X^A_t)\) and \(P_N (A^{1 - \theta} \bar{X}^A_t)\) with coefficient bounded by \(\log (N)\) and \(N^{2 - 4 \theta}\), by following the proof of Lemma 5.4 we get

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in [0, T], x \in \mathbb{T}^2} \| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} (x) \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^2 \lesssim (\log N)^{n_1 + n_2} N^{4 - 8 \theta},\)

and thus, since \(\| K_j \|_{L^1 (\mathbb{T}^2)} = 1\), \(\| V^{j, r}_N \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} \lesssim \| K_j \|_{L^1 (\mathbb{T}^2)} (\log N)^{n_1 + n_2} N^{4 - 8 \theta} \lesssim (\log N)^{n_1 + n_2} N^{4 - 8 \theta}\) (when \(V^{j, r}_N\) is replaced by \(V^j_N\) we obtain a bound proportional to \((\log (N))^{n_1 + n_2}\)).

Following again the proof of Lemma 5.4, we obtain that

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V^{j, r}_{N'}) - T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V^{j, r}_N) \|_{L^2}^2\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle C_{\tau} \sum_{h, \ell_1, \ldots, \ell_{n_1 + n_2} \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \varphi_j^2 \left( h + \sum_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \ell_i \right) \left( \boldsymbol{1}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| h |}{N'} \right) \prod_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \boldsymbol{1}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| \ell_i |}{N'} \right) - \boldsymbol{1}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| g |}{N} \right) \prod_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \mathbb{I}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| \ell_i |}{N} \right) \right) \cdot\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \hspace{26em} \cdot \frac{1}{{(| h |^2 + m^2)^{4 \theta - 1}} } \prod_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \frac{1}{(| \ell_i |^2 + m^2)}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle C_{\tau} \sum_{h, \ell_1, \ldots, \ell_{n_1 + n_2} \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \varphi_j^2 \left( h + \sum_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \ell_i \right) \left( \boldsymbol{1}_{B_{N'} \backslash B_N} (| h |) \prod_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \boldsymbol{1}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| \ell_i |}{N'} \right) \right) \frac{1}{{(| h |^2 + m^2)^{4 \theta - 1}} } \prod_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \frac{1}{(| \ell_i |^2 + m^2)}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \quad + \sum_{h, \ell_1, \ldots, \ell_{n_1 + n_2} \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \sum_{k = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \varphi_j^2 \left( h + \sum_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \ell_i \right) \left( \boldsymbol{1}_{B_{N'} \backslash B_N} (| \ell_k |) \boldsymbol{1}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| h |}{N} \right) \prod_{i = k + 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \boldsymbol{1}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| \ell_i |}{N} \right) \prod_{i = 1}^{k - 1} \boldsymbol{1}_{B_1} \left( \frac{| \ell_i |}{N'} \right) \right) \cdot\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \hspace{25em} \cdot \frac{1}{{(| h |^2 + m^2)^{4 \theta - 1}} } \prod_{i = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \frac{1}{(| \ell_i |^2 + m^2)} .\) (5.19)

Following the proof of [DD03, Lemma 3.2], we get

\(\displaystyle \textrm{(\hyperref[eq:inequalityBesov1]{5.19})} \lesssim 2^{- j \tilde{s}} \left( \| \tilde{\gamma}_{N, N'} \gamma_{N'}^{n_1 + n_2} \|_{H^{1 + \tilde{s}}} + \sum_{k = 1}^{n_1 + n_2} \| \tilde{\gamma}_N \gamma_{N'}^{k - 1} \gamma^{n_1 + n_2 - k}_N \gamma_{N, N'} \|_{H^{1 + \tilde{s}}} \right)\)

where

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rllllll} \gamma_L (x) & = & \sum_{| k | \leqslant L, k \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{i k \cdot x}}{(m^2 + | k |^2)}, & \hspace{3em} & \gamma_{L,' L} (x) & = & \sum_{L < | k | \leqslant L', k \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{i k \cdot x}}{(m^2 + | k |^2)},\\ \tilde{\gamma}_L (x) & = & \sum_{| k | \leqslant L, k \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{i k \cdot x}}{(m^2 + | k |^2)^{2 \theta - 1}}, & & \tilde{\gamma}_{L,' L} (x) & = & \sum_{L < | k | \leqslant L', k \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{i k \cdot x}}{(m^2 + | k |^2)^{2 \theta - 1}} . \end{array}\)

If we choose, \(\tilde{s} < 0\), \(\delta, \delta' > 0\) and \(p', q' \geqslant 1\) such that

\(\displaystyle \tilde{s} > 2 s - 1, \quad \delta, \delta' > (n - 2) (2 - 4 \theta), \quad \delta > (\tilde{s} + 1) - \frac{1}{q' (n - 1)}, \quad, 8 \theta - 4 - \delta' > (\tilde{s} + 1) - \frac{1}{p'} \quad \frac{1}{p'} + \frac{1}{q'} = 1\)

(the existence of the previous constant is ensured by the conditions \(\frac{2 n (n - 2) + 3}{4 n (n - 2) + 8} < \theta < \frac{1}{2}\), and \(0 \leqslant s < \frac{2 n (n - 2) + 4}{n} \theta - \frac{2 n (n - 2) + 3}{2 n}\)), using the Besov embedding Theorem A.3 for the computation of \(\| \tilde{\gamma}_{N, N'} \|_{B^{1 + \tilde{s}}_{p, p}}\), \(\| \gamma_{N, N'} \|_{B^{1 + \tilde{s}}_{p, p}}\), and so on we get that

\(\displaystyle \| V^{j, r}_{N'} - V^{j, r}_N \|_{\mathbb{L}^2}^2 = \sup_{| \tau | \leqslant \frac{3}{4}} \| T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V^{j, r}_{N'}) - T^{(2)}_{\tau} (V^{j, r}_N) \|_{L^2}^2 \lesssim 2^{- j \tilde{s}} (N^{- \delta'} + N^{- \delta}) . \) (5.20)

Since \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) is a polynomial of degree \(n = n_1 + n_2 + 1\), by the hypercontractivity inequalities (5.17) and (5.20) we have that \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t), P_N (A^{1 - 2 \theta} X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) is a Cauchy sequence in \(C^0 \left( \mathbb{R}_+, B^{s - \frac{1}{2}}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}_X^p) \right)\). In order to get inequality (5.15), we proceed as in the proof of Theorem 5.3. Indeed, for any \(p \geqslant 2\), we have, for any \(N' > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \| V^{j, r}_{N'} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p}\) \(\displaystyle \leqslant\) \(\displaystyle \| V^{j, r}_N \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} + \| V^{j, r}_{N'} - V^{j, r}_N \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \lesssim (\log N)^{n_1 + n_2} N^{2 - 4 \theta} + p^{\frac{n}{2}} 2^{- j \tilde{s}} (N^{- \delta} + N^{- \delta'}) .\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle 2^{j \tilde{s}} \left( (\log N)^{n_1 + n_2} N^{2 - 4 \theta} + p^{\frac{n}{2}} (N^{- \delta} + N^{- \delta'}) \right)\) (5.21)

By our assumptions on \(\delta, \delta'\) there is \(\alpha < \frac{1}{2 - 4 \theta}\) such that, \((2 - 4 \theta) \alpha < \nu (\theta, s)\), \(\frac{n}{2} - \alpha \delta' \leqslant \nu (\theta, s)\) and \(\frac{n}{2} - \alpha \delta \leqslant \nu (\theta, s)\), for a suitable \(0 < \nu (\theta, s) < 1\). This mean that by choosing \(N = p^{\alpha}\) we get

\(\displaystyle \| V^{j, r}_{N'} \|_{\mathbb{L}^p} \lesssim 2^{j \tilde{s}} \left( (\log p)^{n_1 + n_2} p^{\alpha (2 - 4 \theta)} + p^{\frac{n}{2} - \alpha \delta} + p^{\frac{n}{2} - \alpha \delta'} \right) \lesssim 2^{j \tilde{s}} p^{\nu (\theta, s)} .\)

By using inequality (5.18) the claim (5.15) follows.

In order to prove the convergence \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (P_N (X^A_t)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \rightarrow \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \mathfrak{Q} (X_t^A) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) and inequality (5.16), we can repeat the previous reasoning by replacing \(V^{j, r}_N\) by \(V^j\). The reason for the arbitrary (negative) Besov regularity \(- \varepsilon\) in this second case is due to the fact that \(\| V_N^j \|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}} \lesssim (\log (N))^{n_1 + n_2}\).\(\Box\)

We observe now that, by the Itô formula,

ZN,h0t
