(5.22)

where we use the notation

\(\displaystyle \chi \cdot \psi = \sum_{j = 1, 2} (\chi^i \bar{\psi}^i + \bar{\chi}^i \psi^i) .\)

Lemma 5.12. Consider \(\frac{19}{40} < \theta < \frac{1}{2}\), under the hypotheses of Proposition 5.10, then the addends in the sum defining the exponential (5.22) (namely \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} P_N (| P_N (\tilde{X}_0 + h_0) |^4) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) etc.) converges, as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\), to some well defined random processes which we denote by

\(\displaystyle \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}, \quad \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} [(X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) \cdot A^{1 - 2 \theta} (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0)] | X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0 |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}, \) (5.23)
\(\displaystyle \left\langle A^{- \theta} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) | (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}, UA^{- \theta} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) | (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^4} . \) (5.24)

Proof. Thanks to Lemma 5.11 and Theorem A.4 (on the multiplication of distributions in Besov spaces), the expressions in equation (5.23) and equation (5.24) can be defined in the following way: taking for example \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), if we (formally) expand the fourth power and using the properties of Wick products with respect to addition we can define \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_t + e^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\)

\(\displaystyle \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) \(\displaystyle :=\) \(\displaystyle \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} + 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 + 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} | \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 |^2\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle + 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^{A, 1}_t X^{A, 2}_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} (\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} \bar{h}_0^1 \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} \bar{h}_0^2) + \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \bar{X}^{A, 1}_t \bar{X} ^{A, 2}_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} (\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0^1 \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0^2)\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle + 2 (X^A_t \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) | \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 |^2 + | \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 |^4 .\) (5.25)

The right hand side of the expression (5.25) is well-defined since \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), etc. are, by Lemma 5.11, random distributions in \(B^{- \varepsilon}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\), meanwhile \(\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0\), \(| \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 |^2\), etc. are random fields in \(C^1 (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\). Since \(- \varepsilon + 1 > 0\) (for \(\varepsilon\) small enough) and \(\frac{1}{p} + \frac{1}{\infty} \leqslant 1\) (for any \(p \geqslant 1\)), by Theorem A.4, the products \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0\), \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} | \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 |^2\), etc. are well-defined as random distribution in \(B^{- \varepsilon}_{p, p} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\). In a similar way we can define all the other random distribution in expression (5.23) and (5.24).

By observing that

\(\displaystyle \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} + 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 + 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} | \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 |^2 +\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle + \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} P_N X^A_t \otimes P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot (\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 \otimes \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0) + | \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N (h_0) |^4,\) (5.26)

it is easy to see that, by Lemma 5.11, Theorem A.4 and the convergence of \(\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N (h_0)\) to \(\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta}} h_0\) in \(C^{1 - \varepsilon} (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\), as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\), the expression at the right hand side of equation (5.26) converges to the sum (5.12), as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\). The convergence of the other terms to the random processes in equations (5.23) and (5.24) can be done in a similar way.\(\Box\)

We can now, prove that \(Z^{N, h_0}_t\) has a limit as \(N \rightarrow \infty\).

Lemma 5.13. Suppose that \(\frac{19}{40} < \theta < \frac{1}{2}\). Then, for any \(p \geqslant 2\) we have that \(Z^{N, h_0}_t \rightarrow Z_t^{h_0}\) in \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\) where \(Z_t^{h_0}\) is as follows

\(\displaystyle Z_t^{h_0}\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \exp \left( \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x - \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_0 + h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \right.\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle + \lambda \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} [(X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) \cdot A^{1 - 2 \theta} (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0)] | X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0 |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} s\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle - \frac{\lambda^2}{2} \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \langle A^{- \theta} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) | (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}, \)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle UA^{- \theta} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) | (X^A_s + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} s} h_0) |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \rangle_{\mathbb{R}^4} \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} t),\) (5.27)

where the expressions \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | (X^A_0 + h_0) |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), etc. in formula (5.27) are defined as explained in Lemma 5.12.

Proof. We prove only that \(\exp \left( \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} P_N (| P_N (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \right)\) converges to

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \exp \left( \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} + 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 + 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} | \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 |^2 + \right.\\ \qquad + \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^A_t \otimes X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot (\mathrm{e}^{- A t} h_0 \otimes e^{- A t} h_0) + | \mathrm{e}^{- A t} h_0 |^4) \mathrm{d} x) \end{array} \) (5.28)

where

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^A_t \otimes X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot (\mathrm{e}^{- A t} h_0 \otimes \mathrm{e}^{- A t} h_0)\\ \quad = 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^{A, 1}_t X^{A, 2}_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} (\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} \bar{h}_0^1 \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} \bar{h}_0^2) + \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \bar{X}^{A, 1}_t \bar{X} ^{A, 2}_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} (\mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0^1 \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0^2), \end{array}\)

in \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\), since the convergence of all other terms can be proved in a similar way. By equation (5.26), in order to prove that \(\exp \left( \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} P_N (| P_N (X^A_t + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0) |^4) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \right)\) converges to the exponential in equation (5.28), it is enough to prove that \(\exp \left( \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \right)\) converges to \(\exp \left( \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \right)\), that \(\exp \left( 2 \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right)\) converges to \(\exp \left( \lambda \int_{\mathbb{T}^2} 2 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right)\) etc.

The convergence of \(\exp \left( \lambda \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \right)\) to \(\exp \left( \lambda \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^4 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathrm{d} x \right)\), has been proved in Theorem 5.3.

If we consider the term \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A t} P_N h_0\), by Lemma 5.11 and Theorem A.4, for any \(k \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(p \geqslant 2\), we have that

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \left\| \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 \mathrm{d} x - \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^{A^{1 - 2 \theta}}_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{k p}}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \leqslant\) \(\displaystyle \| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} - \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \|_{B^{- \varepsilon}_{k p, k p}} \| h_0 \|_{C^1} + \| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \|_{B^{- \varepsilon}_{k p, k p}} N^{(2 \varepsilon - 1)} \| h_0 \|_{C^1} \rightarrow 0\) (5.29)

as \(N \rightarrow \infty\). Furthermore, by inequality (5.15) and Theorem A.4, we get

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \left\| \sum_{k = 0}^n \frac{\lambda^k}{k!} \left( \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 \mathrm{d} x - \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right)^k \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^p}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{k = 0}^n \frac{\lambda^{\lambda}}{k!} \left\| \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 \mathrm{d} x - \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{k p}}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{k = 0}^n \frac{\lambda^k \| h_0 \|_{C^1}^k}{k!} (\| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} - \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \|_{B^{- \varepsilon}_{k p, k p}}^k + N^{k (2 \varepsilon - 1)} \| \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \|_{B^{- \varepsilon}_{k p, k p}}^k)\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{k = 0}^n C_p^k k^{(\nu (\theta, \varepsilon) - 1)},\) (5.30)

where \(C_p > 1\) is a suitable constant depending on \(p\). Since \(\sum_{k = 0}^n C_p^k k^{(\nu (\theta, \varepsilon) - 1)}\) is a convergent sequence, inequality (5.30) proves that \(\exp \left( \lambda \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right)\) is well-defined in \(\mathcal{G}_X^p\). Furthermore, by the convergence of (5.29) and Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, inequality (5.30) implies that \(\exp \left( \lambda \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | P_N X^A_t |^2 P_N X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} P_N h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right)\) converges to \(\exp \left( \lambda \int \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} | X^A_t |^2 X^A_t \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \cdot \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t} h_0 \mathrm{d} x \right)\) in \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\). In the same way, it is possible to prove that the exponential of every term in the sum (5.26) converges to the exponential of the corresponding term of equation (5.28). Since every term in the exponential is even, and thus the standard properties of the products of exponentials hold, and since we proved the convergence of each single exponential in \(C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{G}^p_X)\), for any arbitrary \(p \geqslant 2\), the statement follows from Hölder's inequality for the twisted spaces.\(\Box\)

We have now all the tools for proving the convergence of weak solutions to equation (5.13) to the weak solution to equation (5.10).

Theorem 5.14. Let \(\frac{19}{40} < \theta < \frac{1}{2}\) and \(h_0 \in C^1 (\mathbb{T}^2, \mathcal{G}^{\infty}_X)\). Then, for any \(F \in \bigoplus_{n = 0}^k \Lambda^n (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^2)^r)\) and any \(t_1 < \cdots < t_r \in \mathbb{R}_+\) we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \lim_{N \rightarrow \infty} \omega_0 (F (\chi_{t_1}^{(N)}, \ldots, \chi_{t_r}^{(N)})) & = & \overline{\mathcal{E}}_0^Z (F (X^A_{t_1} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_1} h_0, \ldots, X^A_{t_r} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_1} h_0))\\ & = & \mathcal{E}_0 (F (X^A_{t_1} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_1} h_0, \ldots, X^A_{t_r} + \mathrm{e}^{- A^{1 - 2 \theta} t_1} h_0) Z_{t_r}), \end{eqnarray*}

where \(\chi^{(N)}_t\) is the solution to equation (5.13).

Proof. The proof is a consequence of the representation of solutions to equation (5.13) given in Proposition 5.10 and of the convergence of the process \(Z^{N, h_0}\) to \(Z^{h_0}\) provided by Lemma 5.13.\(\Box\)

Appendix ABesov spaces of distributions taking values in Banach spaces

In this appendix, we want to recall some results about Besov spaces of functions (or distributions) on \(\mathbb{T}^d\) taking values in a Banach space \(\mathcal{A}\). The results of this section can be found in [Ama97, Ama19], where the theory of Besov spaces taking values in a Banach space has been developed.

We denote by \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d)\) the space of smooth functions defined on \(\mathbb{T}^d\) and equipped with the set of seminorms

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{\alpha} := \| | D^{\alpha} f | \|_{L^{\infty} (\mathbb{T}^d)} < \infty,\)

where \(\alpha \in \mathbb{N}^d\). We denote by \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d)\) the strong dual of \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d)\) with respect to the topology induced by the seminorms \(\| \cdot \|_{\alpha}\). If \(A\) is a Banach space and \(B\) a nuclear space, we denote by \(B \hat{\otimes} A\) the completion of \(B \otimes A\) with respect to the natural metric of the algebraic tensor product on \(B \otimes A\). Such a completion is unique up to an isomorphism. Using this notation we define

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{} \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}) : =\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d) \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A}, \quad \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}) : =\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d) \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A},\)

where \(\mathcal{A}\) is a Banach space. It is important to note that

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}) \overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\)

where the arrow means that a space is continuously embedded and dense in the following one.

Remark A.1. Let \(A_1\), \(A_2\) and \(A_3\) be three Banach spaces, for which a product operation \(\cdot : A_1 \times A_2 \rightarrow A_3\) (which is a continuous bilinear function) is defined. Then, for any \(i = 1, 2, 3\), it is possible to define uniquely \(\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle : \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_1) \times \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_2) \rightarrow \mathcal{A}_3\), \(\cdot : \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_2) \times \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_2) \rightarrow \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_3)\), \(\cdot : \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d) \times \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_i) \rightarrow \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_i)\), \(\mathord{*} : \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d) \times \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_i) \rightarrow \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_i)\) and \(D^{\alpha} : \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}) \rightarrow \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) (where \(\alpha \in \mathbb{N}^d\)) which extend in an continuous way the following operations: any \(f \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d)\), \(u \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d)\) and \(a \in A_i\), \(a_1 \in A_1\), \(a_2 \in A_2\) we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rll} \langle f \otimes a_1, u \otimes a_2 \rangle & = & \langle f, u \rangle a_1 a_2\\ (f \otimes a_1) \cdot (u \otimes a_2) & = & (f u) \otimes (a_1 a_2)\\ f \cdot (u \otimes a) & = & (f u) \otimes a\\ f \mathord{*} (u \otimes a) & = & (f \mathord{*} u) \otimes a\\ D^{\alpha} (u \otimes a) & = & (D^{\alpha} u) \otimes a \end{array}\)

where \(\langle f, u \rangle\) is the normal pairing in \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d) \times \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d)\), \((f u)\) is the product in \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d) \times \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d)\), \((f \mathord{*} u)\) is the convolution in \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d) \times \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d)\) and \(D^{\alpha}\) is the \(\alpha\) derivative in \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d)\) (see [Ama19] Appendix 1).

We recall the definition of Littlewood–Paley decomposition on the torus \(\mathbb{T}^d\). Let \(\chi, \varphi\) be smooth non-negative functions from \(\mathbb{R}^d\) to \(\mathbb{R}\) such that

  • \(\operatorname{supp} (\chi) \subset B_{\frac{4}{3}} (0)\) and \(\operatorname{supp} (\varphi) \subset B_{\frac{8}{3}} (0) \setminus B_{\frac{3}{4}} (0)\),

  • \(\chi, \varphi \leqslant 1\) and \(\chi (y) + \sum_{j \geq 0} \varphi (2^{- j} y) = 1\) for any \(y \in \mathbb{R}^n\),

  • \(\operatorname{supp} (\chi) \cap \operatorname{supp} (\varphi (2^{- i} \cdot)) = \emptyset\) for \(i \geqslant 1\),

  • \(\operatorname{supp} (\varphi (2^{- j} \cdot)) \cap \operatorname{supp} (\varphi (2^{- i} \cdot)) = \varnothing\) if \(| i - j | > 1\),

where by \(B_r (x)\) we denote the ball centered at \(x \in \mathbb{R}^d\) and of radius \(r > 0\). We introduce the following notation: \(\varphi_{- 1} = \chi\), \(\varphi_j (\cdot) = \varphi (2^{- j} \cdot)\), \(K_j = \mathcal{F}^{- 1} (\varphi_j |_{\mathbb{Z}^d}) \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d)\).

If \(v \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) and if \(i \in \mathbb{Z}\), \(i \geqslant - 1\) we define the \(i\)th Littlewood–Paley block as follows

\(\displaystyle \Delta_i v = K_i \mathord{*} v \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}) .\)

Then, if \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), \(p, q \in [1, + \infty]\), we define the function

\(\displaystyle \| v \|_{B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})} = \left( \sum_{j = - 1}^{+ \infty} 2^{j s q} \| \Delta_j v \|_{L^p (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})}^q \right)^{1 / q},\)

when \(q \in [1, + \infty)\) and \(\| v \|_{B^s_{p, + \infty} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})} = \sup_j (2^{j s} \| \Delta_j v \|_{L^p (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})})\), where \(\| \cdot \|_{L^p (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})}\) is the norm in the space \(L^p (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) that is,

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{L^p (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})} = \left( \int_{\mathbb{T}^d} \| f (y) \|_{\mathcal{A}}^p \mathrm{d} y \right)^{1 / p}\)

for \(p \in [1, + \infty)\), and

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{L^{\infty} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})} = \sup_{y \in \mathbb{T}^d} \| f (y) \|_{\mathcal{A}},\)

for \(p = + \infty\). For any \(v \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) the norm \(\| v \|_{B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})} < + \infty\) is finite. Then we look at \(B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) as the closure of \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) in \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) with respect to the norm \(\| \cdot \|_{B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})}\). Hereafter, if \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), \(p, q \in [1, + \infty]\), we use the following notation \(\mathcal{C}^s (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}) := B^s_{\infty, \infty} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\), \(B^s_{p, q} := B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathbb{R})\) etc.

In this paper we need some results.

Theorem A.2. Consider \(m > 0\), \(\alpha, s \in \mathbb{R}\), \(p, q \in [1, + \infty]\), such that \(s, s + \alpha \not{\in} \mathbb{N}\) then we have that \((- \Delta + m)^{- \alpha}\), where \(\Delta\) is the standard Laplacian on \(\mathbb{T}^d\), is a continuous linear map from \(B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\) into \(B^{s + \alpha}_{p, q} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A})\).

Proof. This is exactly [Ama19, Theorem 5.3.2] for the case \(\mathbb{R}^d\). The theorem on the torus can be proved in a similar way.\(\Box\)

Theorem A.3. Consider \(s_1 \geqslant s_2 \in \mathbb{R}\), \(p_1, p_2 \in [1, + \infty]\), and let \(A_1 \subset A_2\) be two Banach spaces (where the inclusion is continuous). Suppose further that \(s_1 - \frac{d}{p_1} > s_2 - \frac{d}{p_2}\), then we have the following continuous inclusion

\(\displaystyle B^{s_1}_{p_1, p_1} (\mathbb{T}^d, A_1) \subset B^{s_2}_{p_2, p_2} (\mathbb{T}^d, A_2) .\)

Proof. The proof can be found in [Ama19].\(\Box\)

Theorem A.4. Let \(A_1\), \(A_2\) and \(A_3\) be three Banach spaces, for which a product operation \(\cdot : A_1 \times A_2 \rightarrow A_3\) (which is a continuous bilinear function) is defined. Consider \(s_1, s_2 \in \mathbb{R}\), such that \(s_1 > 0\), \(s_2 \leqslant 0\), and \(s_1 + s_2 > 0\). Consider \(p_1, p_2, p_3 \in [1, + \infty]\) such that \(\frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2} = \frac{1}{p_3}\), then we have that the product \(\cdot : \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_1) \times \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_2) \rightarrow \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_3)\) can be (uniquely) extended in a continuous way to a product from \(\mathcal{B^{s_1}_{p_1, p_1}} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_1) \times B^{s_2}_{p_2, p_2} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_2)\) into \(B^{s_2}_{p_3, p_3} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_3)\) , furthermore for any \(v_1 \in \mathcal{B^{s_1}_{p_1, p_1}} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_1)\) and \(v_2 \in B^{s_2}_{p_2, p_2} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_2)\) we have

\(\displaystyle \| v_1 \cdot v_2 \|_{B^{s_2}_{p_3, p_3} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_3)} \lesssim \| v_1 \|_{\mathcal{B^{s_1}_{p_1, p_1}} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_1)} \| v_2 \|_{B^{s_2}_{p_2, p_2} (\mathbb{T}^d, \mathcal{A}_2)} .\)

Proof. The proof can be found in [Ama19]. \(\Box\)

Appendix BTwisted \(L^2\) spaces and unbounded operators

In this section, we investigate the link between unbounded operators and non-commutative \(L^p\) spaces. Recall that \(\mathcal{H} := L^2 (\mathcal{M})\) equipped with the inner product defined by Haagerup's trace \(\langle x, y \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} := \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} (x^{\ast} y)\) is a Hilbert space, see Proposition 2.11. It is convenient to represent the vNa \(\mathcal{M}\) as acting on \(\mathcal{H}\) by left multiplication, that is, \(\pi_l (x) \xi : = x \xi\), for any \(x \in \mathcal{M}\) and \(\xi \in \mathcal{H}\), giving rise to the so-called standard form \(\{ \pi_l (\mathcal{M}), \mathcal{H}, J, \mathcal{H}_+ \}\) with conjugation \(J \xi = \xi^{\ast}\), see [Haa75]. For the sake of simplicity, we will henceforth write \(\mathcal{M} \equiv \pi_l (\mathcal{M})\). The element \(D^{\frac{1}{2}} \in \mathcal{H}_+\) is the unit cyclic separating vector for \(\mathcal{M}\) associated with the state \(\omega\), so that \(\mathcal{M}\) is in the GNS representation. Recall that \(\mathcal{D} := D^{\frac{1}{2}} \mathcal{M}\) is dense in \(\mathcal{H}\), see Lemma 2.12

Whereas \(\mathcal{M}\) acts as bounded multiplication operators on \(\mathcal{H}\), we would like to extend this identification to elements of \(L^p (\mathcal{M})\) for any \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\) as follows.

Definition B.1. If \(x \in\)\(L^p (\mathcal{M})\) we define \(\operatorname{Op} (x) : \mathcal{D} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}\) by

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Op}_p (x) D^{\frac{1}{2}} y := xD^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} y.\)

This is an extension of the multiplication by elements in \(\mathcal{M}\) since in fact \(\operatorname{Op}_p \left( \mathcal{M}D^{\frac{1}{p}} \right) \equiv \mathcal{M}\), that is,

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Op}_p \left( xD^{\frac{1}{p}} \right) D^{\frac{1}{2}} y = xD^{\frac{1}{2}} y. \qquad \forall x, y \in \mathcal{M}.\)

Unfortunately, the operators so defined lack some important properties, e.g., they might not be closable and this is due to the fact that if \(x_n D^{\frac{1}{p}} \rightarrow x \in L^p (\mathcal{M})\), with \(x_n \in \mathcal{M}_a\), it is not clear whether also \(x_n^{\ast} D^{\frac{1}{p}}\) has a limit, or equivalently if \([x_n]_{\frac{1}{p}} D^{\frac{1}{p}}\) converges.

Lemma B.2. Let \(x \in L^p (\mathcal{M})\) be such that there exists a sequence \(x_n D^{\frac{1}{p}} \rightarrow x\) for which \(x_n^{\ast} D^{\frac{1}{p}}\) is convergent in \(L^p (\mathcal{M})\). Then, \(\operatorname{Op}_p (x)\) is a closable operator.

The proof is the same as for Lemma B.4 proposed below. The difficulty discussed above motivates us consider spaces of unbounded operators where the twisted sequence converges as well, that is, the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) space introduced in Section 2.3.

Definition B.3. Let \(1 \leqslant p \leqslant \infty\). For any \(x \in \mathbb{L}^p (\mathcal{M})\) define the operator \(\mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x) : \mathcal{D} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}\) by

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x) D^{\frac{1}{2}} y := L^2 - \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} x_n D^{\frac{1}{2}} y,\)

where \((x_n) \subset \mathcal{M}_a\), \(x_n \rightarrow x\) in the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) topology.

This definition is meaningful because \(x_n \rightarrow x\) in the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) topology implies convergence of the sequence \(x_n D^{\frac{1}{p}}\). Thus, by Hölder's inequality the \(L^2 -\)limit exists and does not depend on the sequence \((x_n)\). We can prove that such operators are closable.

Lemma B.4. Let \(1 \leqslant p < \infty\). For any \(x \in \mathbb{L}^p (\mathcal{M})\) the operator \(\mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x)\) is closable.

Proof. For any \(a, b \in \mathcal{M}_a\) by continuity we have

\(\displaystyle \left\langle D^{\frac{1}{2}} a, \mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x) D^{\frac{1}{2}} b \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \left\langle D^{\frac{1}{2}} a, x_n D^{\frac{1}{2}} b \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \left\langle x_n^{\ast} D^{\frac{1}{2}} a, D^{\frac{1}{2}} b \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = \left\langle \mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x^{\ast}) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a, D^{\frac{1}{2}} b \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\)

where we crucially used that \(x^{\ast}_n\) converges in \(\mathbb{L}^p\) to \(x^{\ast}\). In other words, we proved that \(\mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x)^{\ast} \supset \mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x^{\ast})\) the latter being densely defined on \(\mathcal{D}\), hence the claim.\(\Box\)

We denote by \(\overline{\mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p} (x)\) the closure of \(\mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x)\) and its domain by \(\mathcal{D} (x) \supset \mathcal{D}\).

Remark B.5. A priori we do not know if \(\mathcal{D}\) is a core for \(\operatorname{Op} (x)\), that is, if \(b\) is another closed operator which coincide with \(a := \overline{\operatorname{Op}} (x)\) on \(\mathcal{D}\) then \(a = b\). In principle there could be a closed operator with a larger domain. We will see below that this does not happen under some additional assumptions.

Let us now investigate sufficient conditions to have self-adjoint operators associated with elements in twisted \(\mathbb{L}^p\) spaces. Recall that if \(x \in \mathcal{M}_a\), \([x]_t^{\ast} = [x^{\ast}]_{- t}\) and this will pose a problem in our computations. This will require some strong condition for proving that a sequence \((x_n)_n \subset \mathcal{M}_a\) self-adjoint converges to a self-adjoint operator on \(\mathcal{H}\).

Theorem B.6. Let \(2 \leqslant p \leqslant \infty\). Let \(x \in L^p (\mathcal{M})\) and assume that there exists \((x_n)_n \subset \mathcal{M}_a\) self-adjoint such that \(x_n D^{\frac{1}{p}}\) converges to \(x\) in \(L^p\) and moreover that \(U_n (t) := \exp (\mathrm{i} x_n t) \in \mathcal{M}_a\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \sup_n \left\| [U_n (s)]_{- \frac{1}{p}} D^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} \right\|_{\frac{2 p}{p - 2}} < \infty . \) (B.1)

Then, \(\operatorname{Op}_p (x)\) is essentially self-adjoint, affiliated with \(\mathcal{M}\) and if we denote by \(\mu\) the law of \(\operatorname{Op}_p (x)\) under \(\omega\), i.e. the unique Radon measure \(\mu\) for which

\(\displaystyle \omega (g (\operatorname{Op}_p (x))) = \int g (y) \mu (\mathrm{d} y)\)

for \(g \in C (\mathbb{R})\), then we have

\(\displaystyle \int y^2 \mu (\mathrm{d} y) = \left\| xD^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} \right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 < \infty . \) (B.2)

Proof. We want to construct an unbounded self-adjoint operator \(X \supseteq \operatorname{Op}_p (x)\) acting on \(\mathcal{H}\) which represent the limit of the sequence \((x_n)_n \subset \mathcal{M}_a\). To do so, we observe that \(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} [U_n (t) U_m (t)^{\ast}] = \mathrm{i} U_n (t) (x_n - x_m) U_m (t)^{\ast}\) and thus, by the fundamental theorem of calculus

\(\displaystyle U_n (t) - U_m (t) = \mathrm{i} \int_0^t U_n (s) (x_n - x_m) U_m (t - s) \mathrm{d} s.\)

Since \(x_m\) is analytic, so is \(U_m (t)\), \([U_m (s)]_{- \frac{1}{p}}\) is well-defined and by assumption \(\sup_n \left\| [U_n (s)]_{- \frac{1}{p}} D^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} \right\|_{\frac{2 p}{p - 2}} \lesssim 1\). Thus, we write

\(\displaystyle U_n (s) (x_n - x_m) U_m (t - s) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a = U_n (s) (x_n - x_m) D^{\frac{1}{p}} [U_m (t - s)]_{- \frac{1}{p}} D^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} a,\)

and by Hölder's inequality

\(\displaystyle \left\| [U_n (t) - U_m (t)] D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} \lesssim t \| a \| \left\| (x_n - x_m) D^{\frac{1}{p}} \right\|_p \) (B.3)

which shows that \(\left( U_n (t) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right)_n\) is a Cauchy sequence in \(\mathcal{H}\), so that we can define \(U (t) : \mathcal{D} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}\) by

\(\displaystyle U (t) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a := \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} U_n (t) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a.\)

By continuity, we have

\(\displaystyle \left\| U (t) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} = \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \left\| U_n (t) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} \leqslant \left\| D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}},\)

so that \(U (t)\) can be extended by continuity to a bounded operator on \(\mathcal{H}\) with \(\| U (t) \|_{\mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}} = 1\). One can also prove that \(U (t)^{\ast} = U (- t)\) and that \(U (t) U (s) = U (t + s)\), the latter as a consequence of the identity

\(\displaystyle U (t) U (s) - U_n (t + s) = (U (t) - U_n (t)) U (s) + U_n (t) (U (s) - U_n (s))\)

where \(U_n (t + s) = U_n (t) U_n (s)\) was used. Thus \((U (t))_t\) is a one-parameter unitary group. We can prove strong continuity: in fact, by continuity and by the same argument that lead us to (B.3), we have

\(\displaystyle \| [1 - U (t)] \varphi \|_{\mathcal{H}} \leqslant \left\| [1 - U (t)] D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} + {\| [1 - U (t)] \|_{\mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}}} \left\| \varphi - D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} \lesssim t \| a \| + \left\| \varphi - D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}},\)

and the r.h.s. can be made arbitrarily small by choosing \(a\) such that \(\left\| \varphi - D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\) is small and then \(t\) small depending on \(a\). We also have, for any \(D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \in \mathcal{D}\)

\(\displaystyle (U (t) - 1) D^{\frac{1}{2}} a = \lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathrm{i} \int_0^t U_n (r) x_n D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \mathrm{d} r = \mathrm{i} \int_0^t U (r) xD^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} a \mathrm{d} r,\)

from which we can define the generator of \(U (t)\)

\(\displaystyle \lim_{t \rightarrow 0} \frac{U (t) - 1}{\mathrm{i} t} D^{\frac{1}{2}} a = xD^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} a,\)

symmetric on \(\mathcal{D}\). By the Stone's theorem, this operator has a self-adjoint extension which we denote by \(X\), such that \(U (t) = \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} Xt}\) and such that \(X \supseteq \operatorname{Op}_p (x)\) and \(XD^{\frac{1}{2}} =\operatorname{Op}_p (x) D^{\frac{1}{2}} = xD^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} \in \mathcal{H}\). Thus, \(X\) is an extension of \(\operatorname{Op}_p (x)\) and furthermore

\(\displaystyle \left\| x_n D^{\frac{1}{2}} a - X D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} \leqslant \left\| x_n D^{\frac{1}{p}} - x \right\|_p \| a \| \rightarrow 0 \qquad \forall D^{\frac{1}{2}} a \in \mathcal{D}.\)

Also, note that \(\mathcal{D}\) is a core for \(X\), since \(X|_{\mathcal{D}}\) is essentially self-adjoint. By the functional calculus we can define \(f (X) \in \mathcal{M}\) for all \(f \in C (\mathbb{R})\) and then

\(\displaystyle \left\| f (X) D^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 = \omega (| f (X) |^2) = \int | f (y) |^2 \mu (\mathrm{d} y)\)

where \(\mu\) is the law of \(x\) under \(\omega\), i.e. the random measure such that

\(\displaystyle \int g (y) \mu (\mathrm{d} y) = \omega (g (X)) = \left\langle D^{\frac{1}{2}}, g (X) D^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\)

for all \(g \in C (\mathbb{R})\). Taking \(f \rightarrow \operatorname{Id}\) we obtain that

\(\displaystyle \left\| xD^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}} \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} = \left\| XD^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_{\mathcal{H}} = \left[ \int y^2 \mu (\mathrm{d} y) \right]^{\frac{1}{2}} .\)
\(\Box\)

Remark B.7. Any self-adjoint operator \(X\) for which \(\int y^2 \mu (\mathrm{d} y) < \infty\) gives rise to an element of \(\mathcal{H}\). To prove this, note that \(f (X) D^{\frac{1}{2}} \in \mathcal{H}\) and

\(\displaystyle \left\| (f (X) - g (X)) D^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 = \int | f (y) - g (y) |^2 \mu (\mathrm{d} y)\)

so if \(f_n (y) \rightarrow y\) in \(L^2 (\mu)\) we have also that \(f_n (X) D^{\frac{1}{2}} \rightarrow x\) in \(\mathcal{H}\). Finally, to see that \(x = X D^{\frac{1}{2}}\), if \(\varphi \in \operatorname{Dom} (X)\) we have \(\left\langle \varphi, f_n (X) D^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = \left\langle f_n (X) \varphi, D^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\) and therefore \(\langle \varphi, x \rangle_{\mathcal{H}} = \left\langle X \varphi, D^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\) which shows that \(D^{\frac{1}{2}} \in \mathcal{D} (X^{\ast}) =\mathcal{D} (X)\) and that \(x = X D^{\frac{1}{2}}\).

We note that (B.2) is particularly interesting in the case \(p = 2\) since it establishes the link between the \(L^2\) norm of the operator and the expectation of \(y \mapsto y^2\). To obtain a link with the \(L^p\) norm, we need to intertwine \(D\) with \(x\) since in fact

\(\displaystyle \int | y |^p \mu (\mathrm{d} y) =\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} \left( \left| xD^{- \frac{1}{p}} \right|^p D \right) .\)

On the other hand, this can be achieved in the twisted setting.

Corollary B.8. Let \(p \in [2, \infty]\) and let \(x \in \mathbb{L}^p\). Assume that there exists \((x_n) \subset \mathcal{M}_a\) self-adjoint such that \(x_n \rightarrow x\) in the \(\mathbb{L}^p\) topology and assume that \(U_n (t) := \exp (\mathrm{i} x_n t)\) satisfies (B.1). Then, \(\mathbb{O}\mathrm{p}_p (x)\) is essentially self-adjoint, affiliated with \(\mathcal{M}\) and if we denote by \(\mu\) its law under \(\omega\), compare with Theorem B.6, we have

\(\displaystyle \int y^2 \mu (\mathrm{d} y) = \left\| xD^{\frac{1}{2}} \right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 < \infty .\)

Furthermore, if \(p = 2 n \in 2\mathbb{N}\), we also have

\(\displaystyle \int | y |^{2 n} \mu (\mathrm{d} y) \leqslant \| x \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}}^{2 n} < \infty .\)

Proof. We only need to prove the last claim. For \(p = 2 n \in 2\mathbb{N}\) we have

\(\displaystyle \int | y |^{2 n} \mu (\mathrm{d} y) =\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} (| x |^{2 n} D) =\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{H}} (T^{(1 / 2 n)}_{\tau_1} (x^{\ast}) T^{(1 / 2 n)}_{\tau_2} (x) \cdots T^{(1 / 2 n)}_{\tau_{2 n}} (x)) \leqslant \| x \|_{\mathbb{L}^{2 n}}^{2 n} .\)
\(\Box\)
